Tropicalized quantum field theory and global tropical sampling
Michael Borinsky

Abstract
We explain how to tropicalize scalar quantum field theory and show that tropicalized massive scalar quantum field theory is exactly solvable. This exact solution manifests as a non-linear recursion equation fulfilled by the expansion coefficients of the quantum effective action. Geometrically, this recursion computes specific volumes of moduli spaces of metric graphs and is thereby analogous to Mirzakhani’s volume recursions on the moduli space of curves. Building on this exact solution, we construct an algorithm that samples points from the moduli space of graphs approximately proportional to their perturbative contribution. Remarkably, this algorithm requires only polynomial time and memory, suggesting that perturbative quantum field theory computations lie in the polynomial-time complexity class, while all known algorithms for evaluating individual Feynman integrals are exponential in time and memory. To demonstrate the capabilities of the algorithm, we evaluate the primitive contribution to the ϕ 4 \phi^{4} beta function at 50 loops with a proof-of-concept implementation.



1
Introduction

1.1
Physical motivation

Quantum field theory is regarded as one of the most accurate theories in science, with predictions that agree with experiments to an extraordinary degree of precision. Yet it remains very difficult to obtain concrete predictions from it. One of the most severe bottlenecks is the need to sum over all Feynman integrals of a given loop order to reach a required accuracy within a perturbation-theoretic workflow. As the number of these integrals and the associated graphs grows factorially, the amount of work necessary to make a quantum field theory prediction at a specified accuracy also grows at least factorially. In contrast, the time, energy, and resources needed to measure such a physical quantity typically grow at most polynomially with the desired accuracy.

From this physical perspective, this article is motivated by the belief that this is a highly unsatisfying situation: a well-formulated law of nature should not only allow for concrete predictions of a physical phenomenon but also enable the observer to make these predictions with lower overall effort than performing the experiment and measuring the outcome.

1.2
Overview of the results

This article addresses this conceptual deficiency of quantum field theory in two steps:

First, we define a deformation of massive scalar quantum field theory inspired by tropical geometry, which we call
tropicalized quantum field theory
.
Our first main result (Theorem
13
) is that this theory is
exactly solvable
:
its quantum effective action satisfies a non-linear partial differential equation, the
tropical loop equation
, that determines all perturbative coefficients recursively. This exact solution holds in any spacetime dimension; however, in the critical dimension, this equation simplifies to an ordinary differential equation.

Second, using this solvability, we design a sampling algorithm that effectively approximates the original perturbative expansion without enumerating individual Feynman integrals.
The algorithm samples points from the moduli space of graphs.
The probability density with which the points are sampled is the tropicalization of the original Schwinger parametric Feynman integrand. The perturbative expansion coefficient of the initial, non-deformed quantum field theory can be computed via a Monte Carlo procedure, with convergence guaranteed by tropical algebra. Crucially, this new sampling algorithm has only
polynomially growing runtime and memory requirements
. The existence of this fast algorithm implies, for instance, that the evaluation of a specific Feynman integral contributing to the perturbative expansion becomes quickly more computationally demanding (with currently available methods) than estimating the global sum over all Feynman integrals at once.

As a proof of concept, we compute perturbative coefficients for the
3
-point correlation function in massive
\phi^{3}
theory in three dimensions up to 20 loops. For
\phi^{4}
theory, we compute some coefficients of the primitive
\beta
-function up to 50 loops.

1.3
Relation to previous works

Over the last decades, there have been many pioneering efforts to simplify and make quantities such as scattering amplitudes in quantum field theory both conceptually and computationally more manageable. Examples include methods that sum all Feynman diagrams of a particular class at once (see, e.g.,
[
48
,
13
]
), as well as the discovery of new, elegant formulations that capture large amounts of combinatorial data (see, e.g.,
[
20
,
3
,
27
]
).

Over the last decade specifically, tropical and convex geometric methods became established tools in fundamental physics (see, e.g.,
[
39
,
7
,
2
,
6
]
).
Panzer introduced the tropicalization of individual Feynman integrals relevant for this work: the
Hepp bound

[
47
]
. The Hepp bound and more general Feynman integral tropicalizations were used by the author in
[
16
]
to give a sampling algorithm for individual Feynman integrals whose runtime scales better than previous algorithms (e.g.,
[
14
]
), but still exponentially with the number of edges of the Feynman graph.

Further, our approach is inspired by the surfaceology program
[
1
,
4
,
5
,
32
,
50
]
. In particular, our perspective resonates with Salvatori’s recent work
[
50
]
, which puts forward a sampling algorithm for surfaces through Hepp-bound-related recursions.
This algorithm allows one to directly compute entire perturbative expansion coefficients of certain quantum field theories. The second part of the present article translates some of these ideas from surfaceology into a purely graph-based setting, without resorting to surfaces at all. The global sampling algorithm presented in Section
4
achieves the same goal of providing a way to compute a full perturbative coefficient at once without resorting to a decomposition into individual Feynman integrals.
Its runtime and memory requirements, which only grow polynomially with the loop order and multiplicity, appear to improve significantly over those of the algorithm in
[
50
]
,
although a direct comparison is not straightforward because the two methods are applied in different settings, namely the
surface-ordered amplitudes in
\Tr(\phi^{3})
versus the
n
-point correlation functions in scalar quantum field theories here.
In reverse, we expect that many of the concepts uncovered in our purely graph-centred setting, such as the exact solvability of tropicalized scalar quantum field theory, have counterparts within the surfaceology framework, and we hope that the present article helps to open a path for a deeper integration of surfaceology with tropicalized quantum field theory.

Recent empirical studies of perturbation theory at large loop orders
[
8
,
10
,
11
,
17
]
provide additional motivation for algorithms that sample quantum field theoretic quantities directly, without explicitly enumerating Feynman diagrams. In particular, Balduf’s analysis
[
8
, §4]
indicates that the distribution of contributions from individual Feynman integrals becomes increasingly heavy-tailed at high loop order. This implies that a naive Monte Carlo strategy—randomly sampling a graph, computing its Feynman integral, and averaging over the results—would suffer from enormous variance and fail to converge efficiently. Such heavy-tailed behaviour makes it unlikely that unbiased sampling over graphs could produce reliable estimates in a reasonable time, showing that more sophisticated methods like the one developed in this work are needed.

The tropical loop equation and the associated non-linear recursion equation for the expansion coefficients
of the tropicalized
n
-point correlation functions have a relative in the theory of matrix integrals: the loop
equations and the more general Eynard–Orantin topological recursion
[
34
]
. It is plausible, but not obvious, that our exact solution of tropicalized massive scalar quantum field theory and the associated non-linear recursion have interpretations as topological recursions.

From a purely mathematical standpoint, the first part of this article proves a formula for the generating function of certain volumes of moduli spaces of metric graphs
[
31
]
(see also
[
28
]
). The necessary volume forms are tropicalized versions of parametric Feynman integrands.
The algorithm in the second part samples points from these moduli spaces uniformly with respect to these forms.
Such volume computations have an analogy in the moduli space of Riemann surfaces, thanks to the seminal work of Mirzakhani
[
45
]
, which also provided input to the surfaceology framework (see, e.g.,
[
1
, §8.2]
).
Her recursion computes the volumes of moduli spaces of Riemann surfaces with fixed boundary lengths.

In the context of amplitude computations, volume forms over similar moduli spaces of graphs have previously been studied by Berghoff
[
12
]
. We also remark that the moduli space of graphs naturally emerges as an infrared limit of the moduli space of Riemann surfaces in string perturbation theory
[
60
]
.

1.4
Plan of the paper

Section
1.5
targets readers who are familiar with the path-integral formulation of quantum field theory. There, we briefly summarize the gist of tropicalized quantum field theory. We recall more specific prerequisites on parametric perturbation theory, tropical geometry, and the Hepp bound in Section
2
.
In this article, we focus on massive scalar quantum field theories in a Euclidean spacetime.
However, there is much flexibility concerning these restrictions, and likely the results of this article can be extended in various ways. We give an overview of weaker but sufficient restrictions beyond the massive Euclidean case in Section
2.2
.
We discuss tropicalized quantum field theory in depth and prove the tropical loop equation in Section
3
. For the proof, we define the tropical effective action in Section
3.1
as a generating function of Hepp-weighted graphs, which we then prove to be equal to the quantum effective action in the tropical limit. We show that the tropical loop equation can be interpreted as a Dyson–Schwinger equation in Section
3.3
and discuss tropicalized quantum field theory in the critical dimension, where the tropical loop equation degenerates to a non-linear ODE, in Section
3.4
. Remarkably, we find that renormalization and tropicalization are naturally compatible.

In Section
4
, we introduce the sampling algorithm and prove its correctness.
There, for the benefit of readability, we will narrow our scope from general massive scalar quantum field theories to massive
\phi^{k}
theory.
We will start in Section
4.1
by interpreting perturbation theoretic problems in massive
\phi^{k}
theory as integration problems over moduli spaces of graphs. In Section
4.2
, we translate the tropical loop equation, the manifestation of the exact solvability of tropicalized quantum field theory, into a non-linear recursion equation for the perturbative coefficients of the
n
-point correlation functions at all orders. This recursion can be solved in polynomial time.
Based on this explicit recursion, we can state the global, polynomial-time sampling algorithm in Section
4.3
, where we also prove its correctness.

A proof-of-concept
C++
implementation of the algorithm is included in the ancillary files of the arXiv version of this article and available as a GitHub repository.
1
1

1

https://github.com/michibo/amplitrop

In Section
5
, we present some illustrative computations we performed with this implementation.
In the final Section
6
,
we outline possible perspectives and generalizations arising from the open directions highlighted throughout this work, pointing toward future investigations.

1.5
A brief overview of tropicalized quantum field theory

A quantum field theory is typically defined by specifying a space of field configurations, which consists of sections of some bundle over a spacetime manifold, and an action functional encoding the dynamics of the theory. A prototypical example is the manifold
\mathbb{R}^{D}
with the trivial line bundle, the field configuration space given by functions
\Phi:\mathbb{R}^{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, and the action functional,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\Phi,J]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D}}\text{d}^{D}x\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Phi(x)\left(\Box+m^{2}\right)\Phi(x)-V[\Phi](x)-J(x)\Phi(x)\bigg),

(1)

which associates a number to each bosonic field configuration
\Phi,J
on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
. We will work with a general scalar potential
V[\Phi](x)=\sum_{k\geq 3}\frac{\lambda_{k}^{\prime}}{k!}\Phi(x)^{k}
with coupling constants
\lambda_{3}^{\prime},\lambda_{4}^{\prime},\ldots
, and the negative Laplacian on
\mathbb{R}^{D}
:
\Box=-\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\mu}
.

Our opening move is to
deform
each member of the scalar QFT family (
1
) via a positive real parameter
\xi
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\xi}[\Phi,J]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D\cdot\xi}}\text{d}^{D\cdot\xi}x\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Phi(x)\left(\Box+m^{2}\right)^{\xi}\Phi(x)-V[\Phi](x)-J(x)\Phi(x)\bigg),

(2)

where we rescaled the spacetime dimension as
D\mapsto D\cdot\xi
and powered the propagator as
(\Box+m^{2})\mapsto(\Box+m^{2})^{\xi}
.
Obviously,
\mathcal{S}[\Phi]=\mathcal{S}^{1}[\Phi]
.
We will use dimensional regularization
[
56
]
. So, the values of the parameters
D
and
\xi
will not necessarily be integers.
It is convenient to pass to dimensionless coupling constants
\lambda_{k}=\mu^{\xi((k-2)D/2-k)}\lambda_{k}^{\prime}
, where
\mu
is an auxiliary mass parameter.

The first message of this article is that we can rightfully call
\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}
the
tropical limit
of (
1
).

Before diving into the precise meaning of this statement, we briefly recall the path integral workflow. We will not use path integrals in our proofs or later in the article. Here, in this section, they are convenient to provide a quick overview of the essence of tropicalized quantum field theory from a physical perspective. We write

\displaystyle Z^{\xi}[J]=\int\exp\left(-\mathcal{S}^{\xi}[\Phi,J]\right)\,\mathcal{D}[\Phi]\,,

for the partition functional that only depends on the source field
J
.
Following a standard quantum field theory stream of logic (see, e.g.,
[
58
]
), we define the
free energy functional
W^{\xi}[J]=\log Z^{\xi}[J]
and the effective action functional,

\Gamma^{\xi}[\Phi_{c}]=W^{\xi}[J[\Phi_{c}]]-\Phi_{c}\cdot J[\Phi_{c}]
,
which is the Legendre transform of
W^{\xi}[J]
with respect to the classical field
\Phi_{c}[J]={\delta W^{\xi}}/{\delta J}
.
The momentum-space
n
-point correlation functions are then

\Gamma^{\xi}_{n}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})={\delta^{n}\Gamma^{\xi}[\Phi_{c}]}/({\delta\Phi_{c}(p_{1})\cdots\delta\Phi_{c}(p_{n})}).

We will show that the
n
-point correlation functions behave well in the tropical limit
\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}
.
Explicitly, we will prove that for Euclidean momenta
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and a positive mass
m^{2}>0
(see Section
2.2
for weaker assumptions, which are also sufficient), we find

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\xi}_{n}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\mu^{\xi(D+n(1-D/2))}\cdot\delta^{({D\cdot\xi})}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\partial^{n}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{n}}\Big|_{\varphi=0}+o(\xi)\right)\text{ as }\xi\rightarrow 0^{+},

(3)

where
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}(D,\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots)
is a dimensionless
function
(and not a functional) in the dimension
D
, the scalar formal variable
\varphi
, and the dimensionless coupling constants
\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots
We will refer to this function as the
tropical effective action
.

The key result of this article is that tropical effective action,
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
, is completely determined by the following partial differential equation (PDE),
which we call the
tropical loop equation
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}\,\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1,

(4)

with the linear, first-order differential operator
\mathcal{P}_{D}
on the left-hand side,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}=\left(-D-\left(1-\frac{D}{2}\right)\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}+\sum_{k\geq 3}\left(k-D\left(\frac{k}{2}-1\right)\right)\lambda_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\right),

(5)

and the boundary data, which is given by

\displaystyle\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\,\hbar\cdot\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}\left(D,\varphi{\hbar}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{3},\hbar^{\frac{2}{2}}\lambda_{4},\hbar^{\frac{3}{2}}\lambda_{5},\ldots\right)=\sum_{k\geq 3}\lambda_{k}\frac{\varphi^{k}}{k!}.

(6)

Eq. (
6
) fixes
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
at tree-level
and eq. (
4
) uniquely determines
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
beyond that (i.e. at loop-level).
These formulas form the foundation of all other results in this article.

Eq. (
4
) is a PDE in infinitely many parameters, which often reduces transparently to a PDE with only a finite number of parameters.
For instance, specializing the tropical effective action to the
\phi^{k}
-theory case, i.e., specifying

\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}:=\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}(D,\varphi,0,\ldots,0,\lambda_{k},0,\ldots)
leads to the PDE

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}^{\phi^{k}}\,\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}=\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1,

(7)

where the differential operator is

\mathcal{P}_{D}^{\phi^{k}}=\left(-D-\left(1-\frac{D}{2}\right)\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}+\left(k-D\left(\frac{k}{2}-1\right)\right)\lambda_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}\right)

and the boundary condition
\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\,\hbar\cdot\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}\left(D,\varphi{\hbar}^{-\frac{1}{2}},0,\ldots,\hbar^{\frac{k-2}{2}}\lambda_{k},0,\ldots\right)=\lambda_{k}\frac{\varphi^{k}}{k!}.

Analogously, we get a finite-dimensional PDE problem if we restrict to finitely many non-zero couplings.

We leave the exploration of tropicalized quantum field theories beyond the scalar and massive case to future works. It even seems possible that gauge theories can be tropicalized. Tropicalized quantum field theory captures only the dominant singular behaviour of the original quantum field theory as a function of the spacetime dimension
D
. Since gauge theories are generally compatible with dimensional regularization, it is plausible that they, too, admit a tropicalization.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Perturbation theory

The (Feynman) graphs typically used in quantum field theory are finite, one-dimensional CW complexes. Our graphs will have no vertices of degree
0
or
2
. Vertices of degree 1 and the adjacent edges have a special role. They are called legs.
We do not allow edges that only connect a pair of legs.
We reserve the name vertex for vertices that are not legs. The edges incident to legs are called external edges, and the term edge is reserved for all other (internal) edges.
A graph is bridgeless if an (internal) edge’s removal does not change the number of connected components. It is 1PI (1-particle-irreducible) if it is connected and bridgeless.
We consider the legs to be labelled uniquely by integers
1,\ldots,n
, and we write
\Aut(G)
for the automorphism group of a graph
G
.
Automorphisms are required to retain the leg labels.
We write

\mathcal{G}_{n}
for the set of isomorphism classes of 1PI graphs with
n
legs.

The
n
-point correlation functions associated with the action (
1
) can be computed perturbatively (i.e. as a power series expansion) by summing over all 1PI graphs:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\left(\mu^{\xi(|v|-{(|v|-2)}D/2)}\lambda_{|v|}\right)}{|\Aut(G)|}I_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}),

(8)

Here, for each of
G
’s vertices
v\in V_{G}
, we denote its degree by
|v|
. The product over
V_{G}
produces the coupling constant monomial associated with the graph
G
. The powers of the auxiliary mass parameter
\mu
are consequences of the choice of dimensionless coupling constants
\lambda_{k}
.
We interpret
\Gamma_{n}^{\xi}
as a power series in the formal parameters
\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots
whose coefficients are functions of
\xi,D,m,p_{1},p_{2},\ldots

The last term
I_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
denotes the Feynman integral associated with
G
. It is given by

\displaystyle I_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{D\cdot\xi}}\frac{\text{d}^{D\cdot\xi}k_{e}}{(2\pi)^{D\cdot\xi}}\frac{1}{(k_{e}^{2}+m^{2})^{\xi}}\right)\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\widehat{\delta}^{({D\cdot\xi})}\left(\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\mathcal{E}_{v,e}k_{e}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}_{v,i}p_{i}\right)

The integration over
k_{e}
for each of
G
’s edges in
E_{G}
extends to the right-most factor,
where
\mathcal{E}_{v,e}
is
G
’s incidence matrix computed by fixing an arbitrary orientation of
G
’s edges and setting
\mathcal{E}_{v,e}=+1/-1
if the edge
e
is directed towards/away from the vertex
v

and
\mathcal{E}_{v,e}=0
if
e
is not incident to
v
.
The matrix
\mathcal{P}_{v,i}
is computed analogously by setting
\mathcal{P}_{v,i}=1
if the
i
-th leg
connects to vertex
v
and zero otherwise.
Here,
\widehat{\delta}^{({D})}(x)=(2\pi)^{D}\delta^{(D)}(x)
is a convenient normalization of the usual
D
-dimensional Dirac
\delta
-function.

Passing to the Feynman parametric representation (see, e.g.,
[
59
, Chapters 2–3]
) and splitting off an overall
\delta
-function yields a compact expression for the
\xi
-deformed Feynman integral

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}I_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\mu^{-2\xi\cdot\omega_{D}(G)}\cdot\widehat{\delta}^{({D\cdot\xi})}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\right)\cdot\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}),\textrm{ where }\\
\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=1\textrm{ if }L(G)=0\textrm{ and }\\
\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G)\cdot\xi)}{\Gamma(\xi)^{|E_{G}|}(4\pi)^{L(G)\cdot D\cdot\xi/2}}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\left(\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\right)^{\xi}\Omega\text{ if }L(G)\geq 1\,.\end{gathered}

Here, the loop number of
G
is
L(G)
, which is equal to the rank of
G
’s fundamental group. As
G
is connected, we have by the usual Euler characteristic formula,
L(G)=|E_{G}|-|V_{G}|+1
.
The
superficial degree of divergence
of
G
is the number
\omega_{D}(G)=|E_{G}|-D\cdot L(G)/2
. Further, the Euler
\Gamma
-function is defined by
\Gamma(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{x}e^{-t}\frac{\text{d}t}{t}
,
the domain
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}
is the
positive part of
|E_{G}|-1
dimensional real projective space
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}=\{[x_{1}:\cdots:x_{|E_{G}|}]\in\mathbb{RP}^{|E_{G}|-1}:x_{e}>0\}
, and

\displaystyle\Omega=\sum_{e=1}^{|E_{G}|}(-1)^{|E_{G}|-e}{\text{d}\log x_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\widehat{\text{d}\log x_{e}}\wedge\cdots\wedge{\text{d}\log x_{|E_{G}|}}

(12)

is the canonical volume form on the projective simplex.
The Symanzik polynomials
\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})
,
\mathcal{F}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})
, and the rational function
\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\mathcal{F}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})/\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})

respect the combinatorial formulas,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})&=\sum_{T\subset G}\prod_{e\not\in T}x_{e}&&\text{ and }&\mathcal{F}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})&=\sum_{F\subset G}\frac{p(F)^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\prod_{e\not\in F}x_{e}+\frac{m^{2}}{\mu^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})\cdot\sum_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e},\end{aligned}

(13)

where we sum over all spanning trees
T
and all spanning two-forests
F
of
G
, respectively, and
p(F)
is the total momentum flowing between
F
’s two components. In contrast to typical treatments, we chose to extract the mass dimension of the polynomial
\mathcal{F}_{G}
for later convenience.
Note that the expression for
\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})

is dimensionless and explicitly depends on
D
and
\xi
.

2.2
Divergences of Feynman integrals and positivity assumptions

The parametric Feynman integral in eq. (
2.1
) is divergent for many graphs
G
and many values of
D
,
m
and
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots
If the external momenta
p_{i}
are Euclidean, we have effective convergence criteria (see
[
54
]
).
In the Minkowskian case,
no such combinatorial convergence criteria exist yet.
Even in the pseudo-Euclidean regime (see, e.g.,
[
19
]
), where all the coefficients of
\mathcal{F}_{G}
are positive, only convergence theorems, which require cumbersome computations involving polytopes, can be applied in general (see, e.g.,
[
16
, Theorem 2.3]
).
A vanishing mass
m^{2}
complicates the question whether a given Feynman integral is convergent or not, and general Minkowski space Feynman integrals can usually only be evaluated unambiguously after a specific deformation of the integration domain via the Feynman
i\varepsilon
prescription (see, e.g.,
[
37
]
).

To avoid these additional complications, we make the simplifying assumption that our kinematic parameters are Euclidean and that the mass of the scalar field is positive
m^{2}>0
. In fact, the weaker assumptions of the kinematics being in the pseudo-Euclidean regime as defined in
[
19
]
or the co-positive cone as defined in
[
55
]
are sufficient together with the
m^{2}>0
assumption or the assumption that the external momenta are sufficiently generic (see, e.g.,
[
19
]
).

Even under all these assumptions, the Feynman integral in eq. (
2.1
) is still often divergent.
However, the divergences are well understood. For instance, for a fixed graph
G
, the integral (
2.1
) exists if the dimension
D
is sufficiently small, and it is a meromorphic function in
D

[
53
]
. A meromorphic function has a well-defined analytic continuation to the whole complex plane, so even if (
2.1
) is only defined for small
D
, we define it for almost all
D
via analytic continuation.

2.3
Tropical algebra

For a polynomial
p({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k_{i}}
,
where
I
is some set of multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{k}}=(k_{1},\ldots,k_{n})
such that
a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\neq 0
for all
{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I
,
we define the
tropical approximation
of
p
as

\displaystyle p^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\max_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k_{i}}.

For example, if
p({\boldsymbol{x}})=x_{1}^{4}+\frac{2}{3}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+10^{5}x_{1}x_{3}^{3}
,
then
p^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\max\{x_{1}^{4},x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2},x_{1}x_{3}^{3}\}
is the piece-wise
monomial function in
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
that is equal to either
x_{1}^{4},x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2},
or
x_{1}x_{3}^{3}
depending on
which monomial is the largest for the given values of
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},
and
x_{4}
. The tropical approximation disregards the
precise coefficients of the original polynomial
p({\boldsymbol{x}})
.
However, the function
p^{\mathrm{tr}}
can change drastically if one of the coefficients is set to
0
.
What follows is a key property of
p^{\mathrm{tr}}
:

Lemma 1
.

If
p
is a homogeneous polynomial in
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
with only positive coefficients, then
there are constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

\displaystyle C_{1}\leq\frac{p({\boldsymbol{x}})}{p^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\leq C_{2}\text{ for all }{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{n},

with
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{n}=\{[x_{1}:\ldots:x_{n}]\in\mathbb{RP}^{n-1}:x_{i}>0\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,n\}
.

Proof.

Let

C_{1}=\min_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
and
C_{2}=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}
.
∎

A stronger version of this statement is proven in
[
16
]
, which also allows for negative
coefficients of
p
.
The function
p^{\mathrm{tr}}
carries the same information as the
Newton polytope

\mathrm{N}[p]\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
p
.
The piece-wise linear function,
{\boldsymbol{y}}\mapsto\log p^{\mathrm{tr}}(e^{y_{1}},\ldots,e^{y_{n}})=\max_{{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\mathrm{N}[p]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{T}{\boldsymbol{y}}
is the
tropicalization
of
p
.

2.4
The Hepp bound

We will show that
we can continuously deform the general scalar quantum field theory (
1
) into a tropicalized quantum field theory, which is perturbatively described by
tropicalized parametric Feynman integrals (see the right-hand side of eq. (
14
) below). Such tropicalized Feynman integrals were introduced by Panzer in
[
47
]
as the
Hepp bound
.

Proposition 2
.

Provided that all coefficients of the
\mathcal{F}_{G}
polynomial are positive,
the parametric Feynman integral can be replaced by its tropicalized counterpart
in the limit
\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}
. That means

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}):=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}}\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\,\Omega\text{ if }L(G)\geq 1\,,

(14)

and

\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}):=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}}\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=I_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=1\text{ if }L(G)=0
,
where

\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\mathcal{F}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})/\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
with the tropical approximations of
\mathcal{U}_{G}
and
\mathcal{F}_{G}
from eq. (
13
).

Proof.

Let
\xi>0
and consider the smooth automorphism
\iota_{\xi}:\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}
given in homogeneous coordinates by
x_{k}\mapsto x_{k}^{1/\xi}
.
Pulling back the canonical form
\Omega
from eq. (
12
) along
\iota_{\xi}
gives
\iota_{\xi}^{*}\Omega=\xi^{1-|E_{G}|}\Omega
.
Hence, using the transformation
\iota_{\xi}
on the
integral expression of (
2.1
) results in

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}_{G}^{\xi}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{\xi\cdot\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G)\cdot\xi)}{(\xi\cdot\Gamma(\xi))^{|E_{G}|}(4\pi)^{L(G)\cdot D\cdot\xi/2}}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\left(\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}^{1/\xi}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{1/\xi})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{1/\xi})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\right)^{\xi}\Omega,

where
\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{1/\xi})
stands for
\mathcal{U}_{G}(x_{1}^{1/\xi},\ldots,x_{|E_{G}|}^{1/\xi})
and
\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}}^{1/\xi})
analogously.

For any polynomial
p({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k_{i}}
with only positive coefficients
a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}>0
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}}p(x_{1}^{1/\xi},\ldots,x_{n}^{1/\xi})^{\xi}=\lim_{\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left(\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}a_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k_{i}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\xi}}\right)^{\xi}=\max_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in I}\prod_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{k_{i}}=p^{\mathrm{tr}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).

The statement follows after using Lemma
1
and

\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G)\cdot\xi)=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)\cdot\xi}+\mathcal{O}(1)
for
\xi\rightarrow 0
.
∎

Panzer’s Hepp bound beautifully captures the combinatorics of the integral on the right-hand side of eq. (
14
). The theory of the Hepp bound extends quite far, touching matroid theory and the number theory of period integrals. Here, we only need basic properties of the Hepp bound.

Definition 3
.

To each graph
G
, we assign the Hepp bound
\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)
, a rational function in
D
by fixing

\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)=1
if
G
is a graph without edges (i.e. a disjoint union of vertices),
and otherwise

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G\setminus e),

(15)

which recursively defines the Hepp bound for all graphs
G
.
(Recall
\omega_{D}(G)=|E_{G}|-D\cdot L(G)/2
.)

Our definition differs slightly from the original one in
[
47
]
,
which defined the Hepp bound via a sum over all permutations and included an overall factor of
\omega_{D}(G)
.
See Proposition 6.8
[
16
]
for a proof that Panzer’s original definition fulfills eq. (
15
).

Our viewpoint on the Hepp bound is similar to Salvatori’s in
[
50
, Section 3.1]
, who put forward an elegant perspective on the Hepp bound that focuses on the identity (
15
). For the reader’s convenience, we will reprove and recombine results on the Hepp bound in this section, both because we will need them later and to keep our exposition self-contained.

Figure 1
:
Factorization property of the Hepp bound

Proposition 4
.

The Hepp bound factors for disconnected graphs and graphs with a cut vertex. Let
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
be graphs,
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
their disjoint union, and
H
the result of gluing together
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
at one vertex.
The Hepp bound fulfills, as illustrated in Figure
1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}(H)=\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})=\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1})\cdot\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{2}).

This follows from the more general Proposition 2.24
[
47
]
(see also
[
50
, Eq. (3.16)]
). We replicate the proof here, adapted to the present notation and specific case.

Proof.

The statement is trivial if
H
,
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
have no edges, as the Hepp bound is always
1
then. We proceed by induction in the number of edges and assume that the statement holds for all graphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
such that
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
has
n-1
edges. For pairs
G_{1},G_{2}
, where
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
has
n
edges, we get from eq. (
15
) that

\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})}\left(\sum_{e_{1}\in E_{G_{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\setminus e_{1}\sqcup G_{2})+\sum_{e_{2}\in E_{G_{2}}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}\setminus e_{2})\right).

On the right-hand side, we may use the induction hypothesis. So,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})}\left(\sum_{e_{1}\in E_{G_{1}}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1}\setminus e_{1})\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{2})+\sum_{e_{2}\in E_{G_{2}}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1})\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{2}\setminus e_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\omega_{D}(G_{1})+\omega_{D}(G_{2})}{\omega_{D}(G_{1}\sqcup G_{2})}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1})\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{2}),

where we used eq. (
15
) again in the second step.
The statement follows from the definition of
\omega_{D}(G)
.
The argument works analogously for graphs
H
as given in the statement.
∎

Proposition 5
.

For a 1PI graph
G
, any set of external momenta
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and
m^{2}>0
such that all coefficients of
\mathcal{F}_{G}
are non-negative, we have

\displaystyle I_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})={\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G).

(16)

This statement also follows from the argument after Lemma 2.8 in

[
47
]
.
We prove it in detail using the recursive Definition
3
,
as the argument will be important later for the discussions in Section
4
.
It might be helpful for the reader to come back to this section while studying the proofs in Section
4.3
.

As Salvatori in
[
50
, §3.1]
, we pass from projective to cubical coordinates in the integral representation of
I_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}
from Proposition
2
.
Let
C_{e}(E_{G})=\{{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}:z_{e^{\prime}}<z_{e}\text{ for all }e^{\prime}\in E_{G}\setminus e\}

be the subset of the
|E_{G}|
-cube,
where the
e
-th coordinate is the largest.

Lemma 6
.

Let
G
be a 1PI graph
and
e\in E_{G}
.
If
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in C_{e}(E_{G})
,
then
\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}({\boldsymbol{z}})=z_{e}\cdot\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G\setminus e}({\boldsymbol{z}})
.

Proof.

For a given set of cubical edge lengths

{\boldsymbol{z}}\in C_{e}(E_{G})
,
where
z_{e}
is the largest coordinate,
we call a spanning tree
T_{\mathrm{max}}
of
G

maximal

if it fulfills

\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T}z_{e^{\prime}}\leq\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T_{\mathrm{max}}}z_{e^{\prime}}
for all spanning trees
T\subset G
.
Assume that a maximal spanning tree
T_{\mathrm{max}}
contains the edge
e
. Because
G
is 1PI,
G\setminus e
is a connected graph.
So, there must be an edge
h\in E_{G}\setminus e

such that
(T_{\mathrm{max}}\setminus e)\cup h
is a spanning tree. This spanning tree fulfills

\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in(T_{\mathrm{max}}\setminus e)\cup h}z_{e^{\prime}}=\frac{z_{e}}{z_{h}}\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T_{\mathrm{max}}}z_{e^{\prime}}>\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T_{\mathrm{max}}}z_{e^{\prime}}

resulting in a contradiction. So, there is no maximal spanning tree that does not contain
e
.
By definition
\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}({\boldsymbol{z}})=\max_{T\subset G}\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T}z_{e^{\prime}}=\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T_{\mathrm{max}}}z_{e^{\prime}}=z_{e}\max_{T\subset G\setminus e}\prod_{e^{\prime}\not\in T}z_{e^{\prime}}
.
∎

Corollary 7
.

For all graphs
G
with
E_{G}\neq\emptyset
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)=\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{\text{d}z_{1}\cdots\text{d}z_{|E_{G}|}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}\,.

Up to a prefactor, this is Salvatori’s formula
[
50
, (3.12)]
.

Proof.

We can apply the decomposition of the cube
[0,1]^{E_{G}}
into subsets
\bigsqcup_{e}C_{e}(E_{G})
(up to sets of measure zero) to the integral on the right-hand side of the stated equation to get

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{\text{d}z_{1}\cdots\text{d}z_{|E_{G}|}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}=\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\int_{C_{e}(E_{G})}\frac{\text{d}z_{1}\cdots\text{d}z_{|E_{G}|}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}.

Applying Lemma
6
under the integral sign and passing to cubical edge coordinates on
G\setminus e
given by
z_{h}^{\prime}=z_{e}z_{h}
for all
h\in E_{G}\setminus e
on the right-hand side results in

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{\text{d}z_{1}\cdots\text{d}z_{|E_{G}|}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}=\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\text{d}z_{e}}{z_{e}}z_{e}^{|E_{G}|-L(G)D/2}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G\setminus e}}}\frac{\text{d}z_{1}^{\prime}\cdots\widehat{\text{d}z_{e}^{\prime}}\cdots\text{d}z_{|E_{G}|}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{U}_{G\setminus e}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{\prime})^{D/2}}.

(17)

We recover the recursive identity (
15
),
because
|E_{G}|-L(G)D/2=\omega_{D}(G)
.
It can easily be checked that the statements hold for graphs
G
where
|E_{G}|=1
. So, the statement follows.
∎

Lemma 8
.

For a 1PI graph
G
, any set of external momenta
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and
m^{2}>0
such that all coefficients of
\mathcal{F}_{G}
are non-negative,
we have

\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\max_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}
.

This statement also follows from the more general considerations of
[
57
, Theorem 1.1]
.

Proof.

For a graph
G
and one of its two-forests
F
contributing to the sum in the formula for
\mathcal{F}_{G}
in (
13
), we can find an edge
e_{F}
such that
F\cup e_{F}
is a spanning tree
T_{F}
of
G
. Hence,

\prod_{e\notin F}x_{e}=x_{e_{F}}\prod_{e\notin F\cup e_{F}}x_{e}\leq x_{e_{F}}\cdot\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
, where we used that
\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\max_{T\subset G}\prod_{e\notin T}x_{e}
with the maximum over all spanning trees of
G
. As
m^{2}>0
, all monomials of the form
x_{e^{\prime}}\prod_{e\notin T}x_{e}
with some spanning tree
T
and edge
e^{\prime}
of
G
appear in the polynomial
\mathcal{F}_{G}
. It follows that

\mathcal{F}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=(\max_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e})\mathcal{\cdot}\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
and therefore
\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\max_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5
.

Recall the formula for
I_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}
from Proposition
2
.
So, by Lemma
8
,

I_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot(\max_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\,\Omega.

Let
P_{e}=\{{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}:x_{e^{\prime}}<x_{e}\text{ for all }e^{\prime}\in E_{G}\setminus e\}
.
We have

I_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E_{G}}\int_{P_{e^{\prime}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot(x_{e^{\prime}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\,\Omega.

There is a diffeomorphism

(0,1)^{E_{G\setminus e^{\prime}}}\rightarrow P_{e^{\prime}}

given by
x_{e}=z_{e}
for
e\neq e^{\prime}
and
x_{e^{\prime}}=1
.
Transforming the integral above into cubical form
using this diffeomorphism
results in the right-hand side of
eq. (
17
), which equals
\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)
.
∎

3
Tropicalized quantum field theory

3.1
The tropical effective action

With all these tools at hand, we are now ready to give an explicit definition of the tropicalized effective action as a generating function of the Hepp bound
summed over all graphs. We will then prove that this definition reproduces the
quantum effective action in the tropical limit.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be the set of all isomorphism classes of 1PI graphs with an arbitrary number of legs, where the legs are now unlabelled. We write
\Aut^{\prime}(G)
for the group of automorphisms of
G
that are allowed to permute the legs and
n(G)
for the number of legs of a graph
G\in\mathcal{G}
.
The tropical effective action
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
is the generating function of the set
\mathcal{G}
weighted by the Hepp bound,

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}(D,\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots)=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)\in\mathbb{Q}(D)[\![\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots]\!]\,.\end{gathered}

Under the positivity assumptions outlined in Section
2.2
(pseudo-Euclidean regime and
m^{2}>0
),
we can combine the equations (
8
), (
2.1
), (
14
), and (
16
) into a formula for the effective action in the tropical limit as given in eq. (
3
), which we officially record in the following:

Theorem 9
.

Assuming Euclidean momenta
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
and a mass
m^{2}>0
, the effective action of the general scalar quantum field theory defined in eq. (
2
) fulfills the tropical deformation limit,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\xi}_{n}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\mu^{\xi(D+n(1-D/2))}\cdot\delta^{({D\cdot\xi})}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\partial^{n}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{n}}\Big|_{\varphi=0}+o(\xi)\right)\text{ as }\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}.

Proof.

A standard argument using the orbit stabilizer theorem implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{n}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{n}}\big|_{\varphi=0}=n!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}\\
n(G)=n\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}}\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut(G)|}\,\mathcal{H}_{D}(G),

where we fix the leg labels in
\mathcal{G}_{n}
and
\Aut(G)
.
The statement follows after using the formulas
(
8
), (
2.1
), (
14
), (
16
)
and confirming the power of
\mu
.
∎

3.2
The tropical loop equation

In this section, we will prove the tropical loop equation (
4
),
which (up to a tree-level boundary term) completely fixes
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
and thereby the quantum effective action in the tropical limit. We start by giving a combinatorial interpretation of (
4
)’s right-hand side.

Recall that a bridge of a graph is an edge whose removal increases the number of connected components,
and that a connected graph without bridges is a 1PI graph.

Definition 10
.

A connected graph
G
, with two or more legs, exactly two of which have a special colour is a
beaded graph
if it is either a 1PI graph or if cutting any of its bridges and giving the special colour to both newly created legs from the cut yields a pair of beaded graphs.

Beaded graphs can be formed by stringing 1PI two-point graphs together like beads on a string. Note, however, that we allow, in a general beaded graph, each 1PI component to have an arbitrary number of non-special legs.
Figure
2
shows examples of beaded graphs. The special legs are drawn as doubled lines. Note that we can string
any number of vertices together to form such a graph. The last graph
in the figure is not beaded, because it has a bridge
whose removal does not separate the two special legs.

\displaystyle\hbox to19.27pt{\vbox to17.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 9.63591pt\lower-8.9359pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.81 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -11.81 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -11.81 0 L 0 0}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 11.81 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Figure 2
:

Five examples of beaded graphs and
one example of a non-beaded graph.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be the set of isomorphism classes of beaded graphs with two specially coloured legs and an arbitrary number of uncoloured legs. For a beaded graph
G
, let
\Aut_{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}(G)
be the group of automorphisms of
G
which do not mix special and non-special legs, but are allowed to permute legs otherwise. Basic generatingfunctionology proves the following formula:

Proposition 11
.

The generating function of beaded graphs weighted by their Hepp bound is

\displaystyle\sum_{G\in\mathcal{B}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)-2}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut_{\mathcal{B}}^{\prime}(G)|}\,\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1\right).

Proof.

Almost all involved combinatorial objects are
labelled structures
from the perspective of enumerative combinatorics and generatingfunctionology (see, e.g.,
[
35
, A.II]
). It is also well-known in quantum field theory that taking derivatives of a generating function passes to the corresponding pointed object (for a combinatorial viewpoint, see
[
35
, A.II.6.1]
). For instance,

{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}/{\partial\varphi^{2}}
is the generating function of
Hepp bound weighted graphs with two or more legs, two of which are fixed and labelled (i.e. pointed).

Recall the factorization property of the Hepp bound from Proposition
4
.
It follows from Definition
3

that for a graph with only a single edge, e.g.,

,
we have

\mathcal{H}_{D}(\hbox to14.38pt{\vbox to3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.19046pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 0 M -5.8 0 C -5.8 1.15 -6.73 2.08 -7.87 2.08 C -9.02 2.08 -9.95 1.15 -9.95 0 C -9.95 -1.15 -9.02 -2.08 -7.87 -2.08 C -6.73 -2.08 -5.8 -1.15 -5.8 0 Z M -7.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 0 M 9.95 0 C 9.95 1.15 9.02 2.08 7.87 2.08 C 6.73 2.08 5.8 1.15 5.8 0 C 5.8 -1.15 6.73 -2.08 7.87 -2.08 C 9.02 -2.08 9.95 -1.15 9.95 0 Z M 7.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=1
,
because

\omega_{D}(\hbox to14.38pt{\vbox to3pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.19046pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 0 L 7.87 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -7.87 0 M -5.8 0 C -5.8 1.15 -6.73 2.08 -7.87 2.08 C -9.02 2.08 -9.95 1.15 -9.95 0 C -9.95 -1.15 -9.02 -2.08 -7.87 -2.08 C -6.73 -2.08 -5.8 -1.15 -5.8 0 Z M -7.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 0 M 9.95 0 C 9.95 1.15 9.02 2.08 7.87 2.08 C 6.73 2.08 5.8 1.15 5.8 0 C 5.8 -1.15 6.73 -2.08 7.87 -2.08 C 9.02 -2.08 9.95 -1.15 9.95 0 Z M 7.87 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}})=1-0=1
.
Therefore, for a beaded graph
H

that is formed by stringing
the 1PI graphs
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots
together,
we have
\mathcal{H}_{D}(H)=\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{1})\cdot\mathcal{H}_{D}(G_{2})\cdots

We can string any number of two-pointed 1PI graphs together along their distinct legs to form a beaded graph.
By the factorization property, the generating function
{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}/{\partial\varphi^{2}}+\left({\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}/{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{2}+\left({\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}/{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{3}+\ldots

of such strings overcounts beaded graphs by a factor of two, because the two endpoints
of the string are not interchangeable. Accounting for the additional 2-fold symmetry and summing the geometric series gives the stated formula.
∎

This proposition gives a combinatorial interpretation for the right-hand side of (
4
). We will continue with the left-hand side, which involves the linear differential operator
\mathcal{P}_{D}
.
The following lemma explains the somewhat ad hoc-seeming shape of this operator.

Lemma 12
.

For any connected graph
G
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}\left(\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}\right)=2\omega_{D}(G)\cdot\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|},

where
\mathcal{P}_{D}
is defined in eq. (
5
)
and we recall that
n(G)
is the number of legs of
G
.

Proof.

By the Euler characteristic formula and the total
number of half-edges of a connected graph with
n(G)

legs, we have

L(G)=|E_{G}|-|V_{G}|+1
, and

\sum_{v\in V_{G}}|v|=n(G)+2|E_{G}|
. Hence,

\displaystyle\omega_{D}(G)=|E_{G}|-L(G)\cdot D/2=-\frac{D}{2}-\left(1-\frac{D}{2}\right)\frac{n(G)}{2}+\sum_{v\in V_{G}}\left(\frac{|v|}{2}-\frac{D}{2}\left(\frac{|v|}{2}-1\right)\right).

Now, replace
n(G)
with
\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}
,

\sum_{v\in V_{G}}1
with
\sum_{k\geq 3}\lambda_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}
,
and
\sum_{v\in V_{G}}|v|

with

\sum_{k\geq 3}k\lambda_{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\lambda_{k}}
.
∎
