Figure 3
:
Illustration of the tropical loop equation.
Each orange blob stands for a 1PI graph.
The right-hand graphs have one
purple pointed edge.
Cutting these edges yields beaded graphs.

With this observation in hand, we are ready to prove
the tropical loop equation (
4
), which we
formally record as follows.

Theorem 13
.

The tropical effective action fulfills the PDE

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}\,\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1.

Figure
3
illustrates this formula and the argument of the proof below.
The name
tropical loop equation
emphasizes that the right-hand side
contains only data of one loop order less than the left-hand side by stringing 1PI graphs into a loop.

Proof.

From eq. (
3.1
), Lemma
12
,
and Definition
3
, we know that

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,2\omega_{D}(G)\cdot{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)=2\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,\sum_{e\in E_{G}}\mathcal{H}_{D}(G\setminus e).

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the set of isomorphism classes of
edge-pointed 1PI graphs
, i.e. pairs
(G,e)
such that
G\in\mathcal{G}
and
e\in E_{G}
.
We write
\Aut^{\prime}(G,e)
for the subgroup of
\Aut^{\prime}(G)
that fixes the edge
e
. Using the orbit-stabilizer theorem, we may switch the
1/\Aut^{\prime}
factor and the sum over
e
in the equation above to get a generating function of Hepp-weighted edge-pointed 1PI graphs:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{D}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=2\sum_{(G,e)\in\mathcal{E}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G,e)|}\,\mathcal{H}_{D}(G\setminus e).

Figure
3
illustrates this equation. The stated formula now follows by observing that the set of beaded graphs
\mathcal{B}
and the set of edge-pointed 1PI graphs
\mathcal{E}
are naturally isomorphic: If we cut the pointed edge of an edge-pointed 1PI graph and give a special colour to the two new legs, we get a beaded graph. If we connected the two special legs of a beaded graph and remember the position of the newly created edge, we get an edge-pointed 1PI graph. The respective notions of automorphisms are compatible. Further, we have
\mathcal{H}_{D}(G\setminus e)=\mathcal{H}(G_{1})\cdot\mathcal{H}(G_{2})\cdots
, where
G_{1},G_{2},\ldots
are the 1PI components of
G\setminus e
.
The statement follows from Proposition
11
.
∎

The PDE (
4
) does not fix

\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
completely. 1PI graphs that have no edges (i.e. vertex graphs) contribute terms of the form

\lambda_{k}\frac{\varphi^{k}}{k!}

to
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
. Such terms are mapped to
0
by
\mathcal{P}_{D}
, as can be seen from Lemma
12
.
Consistently, vertex graphs cannot be formed by connecting the two special legs of a beaded graph.
The boundary condition

\displaystyle\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\,\hbar\cdot\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}\left(D,\varphi{\hbar}^{-\frac{1}{2}},\hbar^{\frac{1}{2}}\lambda_{3},\ldots,\hbar^{\frac{k-2}{2}}\lambda_{k},\ldots\right)=\lim_{\hbar\rightarrow 0}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\hbar^{L(G)}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)=\lambda_{k}\frac{\varphi^{k}}{k!}

fixes the 1PI vertex contribution to
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
to the value required by the definition in eq. (
3.1
).

3.3
Perturbative tropicalized quantum field theory

In this article, we will not attempt an in-depth analysis of the PDE (
4
). It is not
apparent if (and how) it could be solved analytically.
In this section, we will explain how to solve it effectively in the ring of power series
\mathbb{Q}(D)[\![\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots]\!]
in the formal variables
\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots
over the field of rational functions in
D
. By Lemma
12
, the differential operator
\mathcal{P}_{D}
is diagonal in the canonical basis of this ring over
\mathbb{Q}(D)
.
Further, it is easy to see that for
connected graphs
G
and generic
D
, we only have
\omega_{D}(G)=0
if
G
is a single-vertex graph.
So, by the definition of
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
in eq. (
3.1
),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\sum_{k\geq 3}\varphi^{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{k!}+(\textit{terms that lie in the image of $\mathcal{P}_{D}$}).

Therefore, Theorem
13
implies that

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\sum_{k\geq 3}\varphi^{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{k!}+\mathcal{P}_{D}^{-1}\left(\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1\right),

(19)

where
\mathcal{P}_{D}^{-1}
is the canonical inverse of
\mathcal{P}_{D}
in the monomial basis.
We remark that the fixed-point equation (
19
) is a spot-on manifestation
of a
Dyson–Schwinger equation

[
15
, Chapter 19]
. In particular, it can be easily put into the form of the ‘simple example’ in
[
42
, §2]
. This enables the study of this fixed-point equation using the Connes–Kreimer Hopf algebra of rooted trees
[
29
]
. We leave such a study to a future work.

Let
f
be the operator
\mathbb{Q}(D)[\![\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots]\!]\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}(D)[\![\varphi,\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4},\ldots]\!]

given by

\displaystyle f(P)=\sum_{k\geq 3}\varphi^{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{k!}+\mathcal{P}_{D}^{-1}\left(\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}P}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1\right).

It is a small exercise in operator theory on such rings to
prove that with
P_{0}=0
, the iteration
P_{k+1}=f(P_{k})
converges to

\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
from eq. (
3.1
):

\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}P_{k}
in the usual power series topology.
The series expansion of
\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}
can be computed easily using this iteration. The first few terms are

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}=-\frac{\varphi\lambda_{3}}{D-2}+\frac{\lambda_{4}}{2(D-2)^{2}}+\frac{\lambda_{3}^{2}}{(D-3)(D-4)}+\varphi\lambda_{3}^{3}\frac{5D-24}{(D-4)^{2}(D-6)}+\frac{\varphi\lambda_{5}}{2(D-2)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\varphi\lambda_{3}\lambda_{4}\frac{2(2D-5)}{(D-2)(D-3)(D-4)}-\frac{\varphi^{2}\lambda_{3}^{2}}{D-4}-\frac{\varphi^{2}\lambda_{4}}{2(D-2)}-2\frac{\lambda_{3}\lambda_{5}}{(D-2)(D-3)(D-4)}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{3}\lambda_{3}^{4}\frac{-2400+1412D-272D^{2}+17D^{3}}{(D-4)^{3}(D-5)(D-6)(D-8)}-\frac{\lambda_{6}}{6(D-2)^{3}}-\lambda_{4}^{2}\frac{32-25D+5D^{2}}{(D-2)^{2}(D-3)(D-4)(3D-8)}

\displaystyle-\lambda_{3}^{2}\lambda_{4}\frac{480-290D+39D^{2}}{(D-2)(D-4)^{2}(D-6)(3D-10)}+\ldots

3.4
Critical tropicalized quantum field theory

The tropical loop equation (
4
) shows interesting behaviour if we restrict ourselves to one coupling constant, i.e. we put
\lambda_{r}=0
for all
r\neq k
and fix the dimension such that the
\lambda_{k}
coupling becomes critical. Under these restrictions, the non-linear PDE (
4
) reduces to a non-linear ODE. For instance, in the
\phi^{4}
theory case, where
\lambda_{r}=0
for all
r\neq 4
and
D=4
, eq. (
4
) reduces to

\displaystyle\left(-4+\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}\right)\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}^{\mathrm{tr}}=\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1.

Here, an interesting twist emerges:
The tropical loop equation becomes autonomous of the coupling, which suddenly only contributes via the boundary condition. It is unclear how to implement the perturbative
approach from the last section: Now all 1PI graphs with four external legs lie in the kernel of the operator
-4+\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}
, not just terms corresponding to single vertices. Without the regularization provided by a generic
D
, it is suddenly non-trivial to invert the operator
-4+\varphi\frac{\partial}{\partial\varphi}
. The root of the problem lies in spurious perturbative divergences that appear in the double limit
\lambda_{4}\rightarrow 0
and
D\rightarrow 4
. The famous perturbative quantum field theory answer to this issue is
renormalization
.
However, the tropical loop equation elegantly resolves these divergences on its own.
The coupling constant only enters via the boundary data that can conveniently also be interpreted as
renormalization conditions
for the second-order ODE:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\big|_{\varphi=0}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial^{4}\Gamma_{\phi^{4}}^{\mathrm{tr}}}{\partial\varphi^{4}}\big|_{\varphi=0}=\lambda_{4}.

So, renormalization is naturally baked into the tropical loop equation. Also, we could use the first boundary condition to reintroduce a mass term into the tropicalized QFT. A more detailed study of critical and renormalized tropicalized quantum field theory was announced by Balduf and Panzer and will appear in a forthcoming work
[
9
]
.

4
Global tropical sampling

In
[
16
]
, the author introduced a sampling algorithm that randomly generates points in
\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0}
, weighted by evaluations of the tropicalized Symanzik polynomials
\mathcal{U}_{G},\mathcal{F}_{G}
, tailored towards an efficient evaluation of Feynman integrals. Here, we will extend this idea to the entire quantum field theory and directly evaluate full perturbative coefficients in one go. Remarkably, and in contrast to
[
16
]
, the new global sampling algorithm that we will introduce runs in polynomial time. At sufficiently large loop order, it hence becomes significantly more efficient to evaluate the entire perturbative coefficient at once than to compute an individual Feynman integral contributing to it. In fact, due to the different runtime scalings, it will quickly become more efficient to evaluate the whole coefficient than
any single Feynman integral at the same loop order
.

Recall the definition of the
n
-point correlation function in eqs. (
8
)–(
2.1
), which imply:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\mu^{D+n(1-D/2)}\cdot\widehat{\delta}^{({D})}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\right)\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}_{n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}_{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G))}{(4\pi)^{L(G)\cdot D/2}}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\Omega\,,

(20)

where we set the deformation parameter back to
\xi=1
, as we are now interested in the original non-deformed quantum field theory. We aim to compute
\Gamma_{n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
. We will provide an algorithm that does so numerically. This algorithm relies crucially on the tropicalized quantum field theory in the
\xi\rightarrow 0^{+}
limit
and its solution via the tropical loop equation (
4
).

In this section, to keep the notation manageable and for concreteness, we will mostly restrict ourselves to the
\phi^{k}
case, where there is only one type of interaction. The algorithms and arguments presented here generalize to more complicated interactions, but at the cost of a large proliferation of indices for bookkeeping. To avoid this notational blowup, we will fix
k\geq 3
and assume
\lambda_{r}=0
for all
r\neq k
.

Further, it is convenient to pass to indexing by loop order instead of the number of vertices.

Lemma 14
.

A connected,
k
-regular graph
G
with
n
legs and
L
loops has

\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}
vertices and

\frac{(L-1)k+n}{k-2}
edges.

Proof.

Use the identities in the proof of Lemma
12
and

|v|=k
for all
v\in V_{G}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{G}_{L,n}^{k}
be the set of isomorphism classes of
k
-regular (i.e.
\phi^{k}
) 1PI graphs with
n
legs and
L
loops.
In the
k
-regular case, eq. (
20
) specializes to the following formula for the expansion of the
n
-point correlation function:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{\frac{D}{2}(1-\frac{n}{2})}}\sum_{L\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{k}}{(4\pi)^{\frac{D}{4}(k-2)}}\right)^{\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}_{L,n}^{k}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G))}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\Omega\,.

(21)

The numerical evaluation algorithm will sample pairs
of a graph
G
and a corresponding point
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0}
.
We can interpret these pairs as metric graphs: Each homogeneous coordinate
x_{e}

provides a length for the corresponding edge
e
of
G
.
The pairs will be produced with probabilities that are close to the
values of the summand and integrand in eq. (
21
).
Before we dive into the inner workings of this sampling algorithm,
we will provide a conceptually helpful interpretation of
n
-point correlation functions
as integrals over a moduli space of metric graphs.

4.1
Moduli spaces of metric
k
-regular graphs and their volumes

In
[
45
]
, Maryam Mirzakhani
derived elegant recursive formulas to compute the volumes of moduli spaces of bordered Riemann surfaces (i.e., surfaces of given genus with specified boundary lengths).
These volumes are given by integrating the Weil–Petersson form over the moduli space.

The discussion in the preceding sections can be viewed as an analogue of Mirzakhani’s volume recursion. The present section aims to make this analogy a bit more precise: the moduli space of Riemann surfaces in Mirzakhani’s setting is replaced here by a moduli space of graphs, and the Weil–Petersson volume form is replaced by a form determined by the tropicalized Symanzik polynomials that is natural from a quantum field theory perspective. Finally, in this correspondence, Mirzakhani’s recursion equation is replaced by the tropical loop equation (
4
).

Similar to Mirzakhani’s recursion, which not only computes Weil–Petersson volumes but also enables the study of probability measures and sampling of random Riemann surfaces, the tropical loop equation in our framework provides a method to study specific probability measures on the moduli space of graphs. Remarkably, there is also an explicit, polynomial-time algorithm to sample random metric graphs accordingly. This sampling procedure allows for efficient numerical integration of Feynman-type perturbative expansions such as
n
-point correlation functions via a basic Monte Carlo workflow.

The moduli space of metric
k
-regular graphs
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
with
L
loops and
n
legs
is an
orbispace
that combines all graphs with a (normalized) metric into one geometric object.
Let

\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
be the set of isomorphism classes of
k
-regular 1PI graphs with
n
legs and
L
loops.
As a set,
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}=\bigsqcup_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0}/\Aut(G)\,,

where we take the disjoint union over all
k
-regular 1PI graphs
G
with
n
legs and
L
loops and take the quotient with respect to the graphs’ automorphism groups. As in Section
2
, the projective simplex
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}
is the positive part of
|E_{G}|-1
dimensional real projective space parametrized by one homogeneous coordinate for each edge of
G
. The group
\Aut(G)
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0}
via the canonical action induced on
G
’s edges applied to these coordinates.
This action is not free. As an orbispace
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
locally keeps track of the stabilizers of this action.
It is a subspace of the moduli space of graphs and the (compact) moduli space of tropical curves of genus
L
with
n
marked points (see, e.g.,
[
28
]
):
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}\subset\mathcal{MG}_{L,n}\subset\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
.

Each point in
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
is represented by a pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})
where
G
is a
k
-regular 1PI graph and

{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0}
. We can think of such a point as a
metric graph
of fixed total volume, where the homogeneous coordinate
x_{e}
gives the length of the edge
e
. A differential
p
-form on
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
is a collection of
p
-forms
\rho_{G}\in\Omega^{p}(\mathbb{P}^{E_{G}}_{>0})
for each
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
such that every
\rho_{G}
is invariant under the action of
\Aut(G)
, i.e.
\alpha^{*}\rho_{G}=\rho_{G}
for all
\alpha\in\Aut(G)
.
On top of that, it is required that such a family of forms is compatible with a canonical gluing of the cells (see, e.g.,
[
24
, §2.2]
), but these additional conditions are irrelevant here, so we will not discuss them.
Let
\rho=\{\rho_{G}\}_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}
be such a collection of top-forms. Orbifold integration over
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
is defined by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}}\rho=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\rho_{G}\,,

where we integrate over all cells in the moduli space and weight each point by the cardinality of the stabilizer that is attached to it.
A particularly interesting example of an appropriate form comes from
\phi^{k}
quantum field theory. It is given by

\displaystyle\mu_{L,n}

\displaystyle=\{\mu_{G}\}_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}

where

\displaystyle\mu_{G}

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G))\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\,\Omega\,.

We also define the tropicalized analogue of
\mu_{L,n}

\displaystyle\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}

\displaystyle=\{\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}\}_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}

where

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{D/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{D}(G)}}\,\Omega\,.

From the definition of
\mathcal{U}_{G}
and
\mathcal{V}_{G}
it follows that

\mu_{G}
and
\mu_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}
are invariant under the action of
\Aut(G)
, so both
families give rise to appropriate
forms on
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
.

Combining this definition with (
21
) gives a compact formula for the
L
-loop
n
-point correlation function in
\phi^{k}
quantum field theory as a moduli space integral:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}}\mu_{L,n}.

If the integral
of

\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
over

\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}

exists,
then we can define its normalized version,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}=\frac{\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\,,\text{ where }Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)=\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}}\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}\,,

(22)

is an appropriate normalization factor. Positivity of the form
\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
then implies that

\widetilde{\mu}_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}
defines a probability measure on
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
.

Proposition 15
.

If for all
L\geq 1
and
n\geq 2
, where

\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}

and
\frac{(L-1)k+n}{k-2}
are integers,

\displaystyle\omega^{k}_{D}(L,n):=\frac{(L-1)k+n}{k-2}-\frac{LD}{2}>0\,,

(23)

then
the integral in eq. (
22
)
exist and
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
is finite and non-negative
for all
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
.

By Lemma
1
, this proposition
also implies that
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})

is finite provided that the conditions on
m^{2}
and
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
laid out in Section
2.2
are fulfilled. We will postpone the proof of this proposition to the next section.

Using the results of the preceding sections, in particular the tropical loop equation, we will construct an efficient sampling algorithm that produces representatives of points
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
with probability density
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
. Here, efficient means that this algorithm runs in polynomial time and it has polynomially bounded memory requirements.

In the remainder of this section, we will briefly describe the application of this algorithm to numerical integration over the moduli space of graphs and thereby the computation of perturbation theoretic quantities in quantum field theories such as
n
-point correlation functions.
It is convenient to define the residual function
f_{L,n}:\mathcal{MG}_{L,n}^{k}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle f_{L,n}(G,x)=\Gamma(\omega_{D}(G)+1)\left(\frac{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{D/2}\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{\omega_{D}(G)}.

(24)

As before with the top-forms
\mu_{L,n}
and
\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
,
the function
f
descends to a function on
\mathcal{MG}_{L,n}^{k}
, because of the symmetries of the Symanzik polynomials
under the group action of
\Aut(G)
.

Using (
21
) and the definitions above, we find the following representation for the
n
-point correlation function in
\phi^{k}
-theory as an expectation value of the random variable given by
f_{L,n}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})=Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})\cdot\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}}f_{L,n}\cdot\widetilde{\mu}_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}=Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[f_{L,n}\right]_{\mu_{L,n}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\,.

It follows from Lemma
1
and eq. (
24
) that
there exist constants
C_{1}(L,n)
and
C_{2}(L,n)

such that for all
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
, we have

C_{1}(L,n)\leq f_{L,n}(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\leq C_{2}(L,n)
.
This provides all the necessary ingredients to evaluate
the correlation function

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
in one go as an integral over the moduli space of graphs:

Proposition 16
.

If
(G_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,(G_{N},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{N})
are random pairs
(G_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k})\in\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}

distributed independently following the probability density
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
on
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
,
then we can estimate

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,n}^{1}(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\approx\frac{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}f_{L,n}(G_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}).

For sufficiently large
N
, the error of this estimate follows a normal distribution with mean
0

and variance
C({L,n})/N
, where
C({L,n})
is a constant independent of
N
.

Proof.

Follows from a standard argument based on the central limit theorem
(see, e.g.,
[
16
, §2.2]
for an explanation in a related context).
∎

4.2
Explicit recursions for tropicalized quantum field theory

The first and most crucial step in stating a global sampling algorithm for the measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
is to give an efficient way to compute the normalization constant
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
for fixed
D\in\mathbb{R}
.
To do so, we first write
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
as a Hepp bound weighted graph sum:

\displaystyle Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)}{|\Aut(G)|},

which follows, e.g., from Proposition
5
and the definitions of

Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})

and
integration over
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
.
Here, we remark on another advantage of taking a global instead of a local graph-by-graph viewpoint: For a single graph
G
, it costs an with
L
exponentially growing amount of runtime and memory to compute
\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)

[
47
, Corollary 3.10]
.
Here, in our global setup, we can compute the sum over all Hepp bounds
Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})
recursively using the tropical loop equation in polynomial time, as we will now explain.

In Section
3
, we established that the numbers
Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})

form the coefficients of the tropical effective action (see eq. (
3.1
)).
In our, to the
\phi^{k}
theory restricted case, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}(\varphi,0,\ldots,0,\lambda_{k},0,\ldots)=\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\sum_{L\geq 0}Z^{k}_{D}({L,n})\frac{\varphi^{n}}{n!}\lambda_{k}^{\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}},

(25)

where we set
\lambda_{r}=0
for all
r\neq k
and the factor
n!
accounts for passing from labelled legs to unlabelled legs, as we did in the argument for Theorem
9
. Recall Lemma
14
, which implies that

\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}
is always an integer.

In analogy to
\mathcal{G}_{L,n}^{k}
,
we define
\mathcal{B}_{L,n}^{k}
, the set of isomorphism classes of
k
-regular beaded graphs (see Definition
10
) with
n
labelled legs and
L
loops. We also require the two special legs to be distinctly labelled. We define a second set of coefficients
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)\in\mathbb{R}
via

\displaystyle B^{k}_{D}(L,n)=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{B}_{L,n}^{k}}\frac{\mathcal{H}_{D}(G)}{|\Aut_{\mathcal{B}}(G)|},

where
\Aut_{\mathcal{B}}(G)=\Aut(G)
is the group of automorphisms of
G
that fixes all legs.
From Proposition
11
and Lemma
14
, we know that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}=\sum_{n\geq 2}\sum_{L\geq 0}B^{k}_{D}({L,n})\frac{\varphi^{n-2}}{2(n-2)!}\lambda_{k}^{\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}},

(26)

where again the additional factor
2(n-2)!
accounts for fixing the legs.

By the tropical loop equation, as, e.g., given in eq. (
7
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\phi^{k}}_{D}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}=\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1.

(27)

By Lemma
12
,
\mathcal{P}^{\phi^{k}}_{D}\varphi^{n}\lambda_{k}^{m}=2\omega^{k}_{D}(L,n)\,\varphi^{n}\lambda_{k}^{m}
, where
m=\frac{2(L-1)+n}{k-2}
and
with
\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)
from eq. (
23
).
We have
\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)=\omega_{D}(G)
for all
G\in\mathcal{G}_{L,n}^{k}
, by Lemma
14
.
By combining this with eqs. (
25
), (
26
), and (
27
), we get a relation between the coefficients
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)
and
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
,

\displaystyle Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)

\displaystyle=\frac{B^{k}_{D}(L-1,n+2)}{2\omega^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\text{ for all }n\geq 0\text{ and }L\geq 1,

(28)

which allows to compute
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
for all
n
if

B^{k}_{D}(L-1,n^{\prime})
is known for all values of
n^{\prime}
.
The boundary condition for the tropical loop equation
fixes the
L=0
case, i.e. the values
Z^{k}_{D}(0,n)
for all
n\geq 0
.
In our
\phi^{k}
case, we find

Z^{k}_{D}(0,k)=1
and
Z^{k}_{D}(0,m)=0
for all
m\neq k
.
The identity

\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\left(\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\phi^{k}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\right)

translates into the following recursion equation that computes
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)
for all
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
,
provided that
Z^{k}_{D}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is known for all
L^{\prime}\leq L
and
n^{\prime}\leq n
:

\displaystyle B^{k}_{D}(L,n)=Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)+\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{n-2}\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L}\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}Z^{k}_{D}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)B^{k}_{D}(L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime}).

(29)

The recursion terminates, because
Z^{k}_{D}(0,n)=B^{k}_{D}(0,n)=0
for all
n<k
.

Combining eqs. (
28
) and (
29
) gives a loop-by-loop recursion and thereby an efficient algorithm to compute
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
.
Counting the number of steps needed to compute
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
shows that the required computing time scales as
\mathcal{O}(L^{2}(L+n)^{2})
while the required memory scales as
\mathcal{O}(L(L+n))
.

Theorem 17
.

For fixed
D
, the value of
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
can be computed in
polynomial time and memory in
L
and
n
using the recursion equations (
28
) and (
29
).

Proof of Proposition
15
.

Eqs. (
28
) and (
29
)
together with the stated inequality (
23
) imply that
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
and
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)
are finite and non-negative
for all
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
.
∎

(b)

Illustration of the
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
case in Algorithm
19
.

Figure 4
:

Illustrations of the algorithms.
The orange diagonal hatchings indicate 1PI graphs.
The blue dotted pattern stands for beaded graphs.

We are now ready to state the global sampling algorithm
that produces pairs
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})
distributed as the
measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
defined in eq. (
22
).
The strategy is to follow the steps of the recursive computation of the normalization factors
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)
and
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)
from the last section and lift these recursions to integrals over graph moduli spaces.
The algorithm consists of two routines
that invoke each other recursively.
The first routine randomly generates a metric 1PI graph.
The second one randomly generates a metric beaded graph.

Algorithm 18

(Sampling a 1PI graph)
.

Input:
Integers
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 0
such that
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)>0
.

Output:
A pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
of a 1PI graph
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
and an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
.

First, if
L=0
and
n=k
, then return
the pair
(V_{k},{\boldsymbol{z}})
formed by the
k
-vertex graph
V_{k}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}=\emptyset
.
If
L\geq 1
, then follow the steps:

1.

Randomly sample a pair
(H,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})
of a beaded graph
H\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-1,n+2}

with
L-1
loops,
n+2
legs and an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}\in[0,1]^{E_{H}}
using Algorithm
19
.

2.

Form a new graph
G
from
H
by adding a new edge
e^{\prime}
that connects both special legs.

3.

Uniformly draw a random real number
\lambda\in[0,1]
and set
\kappa=\lambda^{1/\omega_{D}(L,n)}
with
\omega_{D}(L,n)
given as in eq. (
23
).

4.

Form an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
by setting
z_{e}=\kappa\cdot z_{e}^{H}
for
e\in E_{H}
and

z_{e^{\prime}}=\kappa
.

5.

Return
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
.

Figure
4(a)
illustrates the essence of Algorithm
18
: gluing together the special legs of a beaded graph. The new graph’s edge parameters are rescaled such that the new edge’s (depicted in purple) parameter is the largest.

Algorithm 19

(Sampling a beaded graph)
.

Input:
Integers
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
such that
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)>0
.

Output:
A pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
of a graph
G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}
and an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
.

Randomly pick one of
1+(L+1)(n-1)
outcomes with different probabilities:

•

Pick outcome
\star
with probability
\frac{Z_{D}^{k}(L,n)}{B_{D}^{k}(L,n)}
.

•

For each of the
(L+1)(n-1)
pairs of integers
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
where
0\leq L^{\prime}\leq L
and
0\leq n^{\prime}\leq n-2
,
pick outcome
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with probability

\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}\frac{Z_{D}^{k}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)B_{D}^{k}(L-L^{\prime},n^{\prime}-n)}{B_{D}^{k}(L,n)}
.

Depending on the picked outcome, follow different steps:

In case of outcome
\star
:

1.

Randomly sample a pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
of a graph
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
and an element
z\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
using Algorithm
18
.

2.

Declare the first two legs of
G
to have the special colour, promoting
G
to a beaded graph.

3.

Return
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
as output.

In case of outcome
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
follow the steps:

1.

Randomly sample a pair
(A,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{A})
of a 1PI graph
A\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2}
with
L^{\prime}
loops and
n^{\prime}+2
legs and an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{A}\in[0,1]^{E_{A}}
using Algorithm
18
.

2.

Randomly sample a pair
(B,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B})
of a beaded graph
B\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime}}

with
L-L^{\prime}
loops and
n-n^{\prime}
legs
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B}\in[0,1]^{E_{B}}
using (the current) Algorithm
19
.

3.

Randomly and uniformly sample a subset
S
of size
n^{\prime}
of
\{3,\ldots,n\}
.

4.

Assign the numbers in
S
to all legs of
A
labelled
\geq 3
in the unique, order-preserving way.

5.

Analogously, assign the numbers
\{3,\ldots,n\}\setminus S
to the non-special legs of
B
.

6.

Form a new graph
G
starting with the disjoint union of
A
and
B
.
Connect the second leg of
A
with the first special leg of
B
via a new edge
e^{\prime}
.
Declare the first leg of
A
to be special such that
G
becomes a beaded graph with
legs labelled by
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

7.

Randomly and uniformly sample a real number
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

8.

Assign the values in
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{A}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B}
to
the corresponding edge in
G
and
the
\lambda
to the new edge
e^{\prime}
,

z_{e^{\prime}}=\lambda
. This way, form an element
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
.

9.

Return the pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
.

The initial discrete sampling step leading to the different outcomes
\star
and
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
is well-posed by eq. (
29
) and Proposition
15
which guarantee that the total probability of picking any outcome is 1.
Figure
4(b)
illustrates the essence of Algorithm
19
: concatenating a 1PI graph with a beaded graph by gluing one of the beaded graph’s special legs to one of the 1PI graph’s legs and declaring another one of the 1PI graph’s legs to be the new special leg.

Theorem 20
.

We assume that the conditions of Proposition
15
are fulfilled.
For given input
L,n\geq 0
such that
Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)>0
, Algorithm
18
outputs the pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
, where
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
with probability density

\displaystyle\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}.

Theorem 21
.

We assume that the conditions of Proposition
15
are fulfilled.
For given input
L\geq 0
,
n\geq 2
such that
B^{k}_{D}(L,n)>0
, Algorithm
19
outputs the pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
where
G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
with probability density

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}.

While studying the proofs of these theorems below, it may be instructive to occasionally return to Section
2.4
to review the necessary integral manipulations that are used heavily here.

Lemma 22
.

For input pairs
(L,n)
where
L=0
, Theorem
20
holds.
If Theorem
21
holds for the input pair
(L-1,n+2)
where
L\geq 1
, then
Theorem
20
holds for
(L,n)
.

Proof.

For
L=0
, Theorem
20
holds trivially,
because Algorithm
18
only outputs the vertex graph without any edge parameters.

Let
f
be a test function on the set of pairs
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
where
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
. We will compute the expectation value of
f
evaluated on a random pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
that is produced by Algorithm
18
.
For input pairs
(L,n)
with
L\geq 1
, Algorithm
18
invokes Algorithm
19
with the input pair
(L-1,n+2)
.
By assumption of the validity of Algorithm
19
for this input and by translating the listed steps of Algorithm
18
into an integral formula, we get the expectation value

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-1,n+2}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(H)|}\int_{0}^{1}\text{d}\lambda\int_{[0,1]^{E_{H}}}f(G(H),{\boldsymbol{z}}(\lambda^{1/\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}))\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{H}}\text{d}z_{e}^{H}}{\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})^{D/2}},

where
G(H)
is the 1PI graph obtained from
H
as described in Step 2 of Algorithm
18
,
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})
is the element of
[0,1]^{E_{G(H)}}
obtained from
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}
and
\kappa=\lambda^{1/\omega^{k}_{D}(L,n)}
as described in Step 4.
From eq. (
28
), and the change of variables
to
\kappa
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{H\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-1,n+2}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(H)|}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\text{d}\kappa}{\kappa}\kappa^{\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{H}}}f(G(H),{\boldsymbol{z}}(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}))\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{H}}\text{d}z_{e}^{H}}{\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})^{D/2}}.

Let
\mathcal{EG}_{L,n}^{k}
be the set of pairs
(G,e)
of a graph
G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
and an edge
e\in E_{G}
up to isomorphism, and
\Aut(G,e)\subset\Aut(G)
the subgroup of automorphisms of
G
that fix
e
. As in the proof of Theorem
13
, we may pass from summing over all
beaded graphs
\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-1,n+2}
to edge-pointed
graphs in
\mathcal{EG}^{k}_{L,n}
.
We get an extra factor of
2
by accounting for the additional
2
-fold symmetry of edge-pointed graphs, where we can change the order of the two legs that were glued together:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{(G,e^{\prime})\in\mathcal{EG}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G,e^{\prime})|}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\text{d}\kappa}{\kappa}\kappa^{\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{H}}}f(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}))\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{H}}\text{d}z_{e}^{H}}{\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})^{D/2}},

where we made the dependence of
{\boldsymbol{z}}={\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})
on the edge that is added to
H
to form
G
explicit, and we keep in mind that
H
is equal to
G\setminus e^{\prime}
.
Using the orbit stabilizer theorem allows us to pass from edge-pointed graphs to 1PI by summing over all edges of
G
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E_{G}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\text{d}\kappa}{\kappa}\kappa^{\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{H}}}f(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H}))\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{H}}\text{d}z_{e}^{H}}{\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})^{D/2}}.

The function
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}:(0,1)\times[0,1]^{E_{H}}\rightarrow[0,1]^{E_{G}},(\kappa,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{H})\mapsto{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}
is given explicitly
by
z_{e}^{e^{\prime}}=\kappa z^{H}_{e}
for all
e\in E_{H}
and
z_{e^{\prime}}^{e^{\prime}}=\kappa
. The image of this injective
map is the set
C_{e^{\prime}}(E_{G})\subset[0,1]^{E_{G}}

as defined before Lemma
6
.
Using this lemma,

\omega_{D}^{k}(L,n)=|E_{G}|-L(G)D/2
,

\deg\mathcal{U}_{H}=L-1
,
and passing to the
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}}
coordinates leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E_{G}}\int_{C_{e^{\prime}}(E_{G})}f(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}})\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z^{e^{\prime}}_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{e^{\prime}})^{D/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}f(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})\cdot\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}.

We used that the sets
C_{e^{\prime}}(E_{G})
for
e^{\prime}\in E_{G}
partition
[0,1]^{E_{G}}
up to nullsets as in Corollary
7
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
21
.

We prove the statement by induction.
Fix a pair
(L,n)
with
L\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
. We assume that Theorem
21
holds for all pairs
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
L^{\prime}\leq L
and either
n^{\prime}<n
or
L^{\prime}<L
.
We will invoke Algorithm
19
for such a pair
(L,n)
.

Let
f
be a test function on the set of pairs
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
where
G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in[0,1]^{E_{G}}
. Consider the probability path associated with the outcome
\star
first. While executing the steps
\star
of Algorithm
19
, we need to invoke Algorithm
18
with the input pair
(L,n)
. By Lemma
22
, we may use Theorem
20
for this input, as Theorem
21
is assumed to be valid for all pairs
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
where
L^{\prime}<L
. The contribution to the expectation value
\mathbb{E}[f]
is simply

\displaystyle E_{\star}=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}f(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}},

where, in the argument to
f
, we interpret the 1PI graph
G
as a beaded graph by giving the special colour to the first two legs.

Next, we look into the case of the probability paths for the outcomes
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
of Algorithm
19
.
These paths will be chosen with probability

\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}{Z_{D}^{k}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)B_{D}^{k}(L-L^{\prime},n^{\prime}-n)}/{B_{D}^{k}(L,n)}
.

Under the induction hypothesis, and by Lemma
22
, we may assume the validity of Theorem
20
for all pairs
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
with
L^{\prime}\leq L
.
The value of
Z_{D}^{k}(0,r)
is zero if
r<k
, because there are no
k
-regular tree graphs with fewer than
k
legs. Hence, we can assume that either
L^{\prime}\geq 1
or
n^{\prime}+2\geq k\geq 3
, as the other cases will only be invoked with probability
0
.
The probability path
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
invokes Algorithm
19
recursively with the input
L-L^{\prime}
and
n-n^{\prime}
.
Because either
L^{\prime}\geq 1
or
n^{\prime}+2\geq 3
, we may assume the validity of this algorithm for the respective input
under the induction hypothesis.

For given
L^{\prime},n^{\prime}
, a 1PI graph
A\in{\mathcal{G}}^{k}_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2}
,
a beaded graph
B\in{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime}}

and a subset
S\subset\{3,\ldots,n\}
,
denote
G(A,B,S)
the graph formed by Step 6 of the
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
part of Algorithm
19
.
For given
{\boldsymbol{z}}^{A}\in[0,1]^{E_{A}},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B}\in[0,1]^{E_{B}}
and
\lambda\in[0,1]
,
write
{\boldsymbol{z}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{A},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B},\lambda)

for the point in the cube
[0,1]^{E_{G}}

obtained via Step 8 of this part of the algorithm.
The contribution to the expectation value
\mathbb{E}[f]
is hence

\displaystyle E_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)}\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{G}}^{k}_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(A)|}\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime})}\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{|\Aut(B)|}\times

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subset\{3,\ldots,n\}\\
|S|=n^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{A}}}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{B}}}\int_{0}^{1}f\left(G(A,B,S),{\boldsymbol{z}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{A},{\boldsymbol{z}}^{B},\lambda)\right)\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{A}}\text{d}z_{e}^{A}}{\mathcal{U}_{A}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{A})^{D/2}}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{B}}\text{d}z_{e}^{B}}{\mathcal{U}_{B}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{B})^{D/2}}\text{d}\lambda\,.

Because the legs of
A
and
B
are fixed,

|\Aut(A)|\cdot|\Aut(B)|=|\Aut(G(A,B,S))|
.
Because all spanning trees of
G(A,B,S)
have
to pass through the bridge connecting the subgraphs

A
and
B
, we also find that

\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{A}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{A})\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{B}({\boldsymbol{z}}^{B})=\mathcal{U}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{G(A,B,S)}({\boldsymbol{z}}).

Hence, this contribution equals

\displaystyle E_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)}\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime})}\frac{1}{\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}}\sum_{A\in{\mathcal{G}}^{k}_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2}}\sum_{B\in{\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subset\{3,\ldots,n\}\\
|S|=n^{\prime}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\times\frac{1}{|\Aut(G(A,B,S))|}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G(A,B,S)}}}f\left(G(A,B,S),{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G(A,B,S)}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G(A,B,S)}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}\,.

Let
\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}
be the set of beaded graphs with
L
loops and
n
legs,
which have at least one bridge, and which
decompose into a 1PI graph with
L^{\prime}
loops and
n^{\prime}+2

legs and a smaller beaded graph if the bridge that is closest to the first leg is removed.
The construction under Step 6 of the
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
part of Algorithm
19
provides a bijection
between
\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
and triples

(A,B,S)
where
A\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2},B\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L-L^{\prime},n-n}
and
S\subset\{3,\ldots,n\}
with
|S|=n^{\prime}-2
. Hence,

\displaystyle E_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{Z^{k}_{D}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)}\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L-L^{\prime},n-n^{\prime})}\frac{1}{\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{f\left(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}\,.

Summing over all outcomes,
\star
and
(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
, weighted by their respective probabilities, results in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{Z^{k}_{D}(L,n)}{B^{k}_{D}(L,n)}E_{\star}+\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L}\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{n-2}\binom{n-2}{n^{\prime}}\frac{Z_{D}^{k}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime}+2)B_{D}^{k}(L-L^{\prime},n^{\prime}-n)}{B_{D}^{k}(L,n)}E_{L^{\prime},n^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{f\left(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{L^{\prime}=0}^{L}\sum_{n^{\prime}=0}^{n-2}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{f\left(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}\,.

Every beaded graph in
\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}

corresponds either to a 1PI graph
in
\mathcal{G}^{k}_{L,n}
or
a beaded graph of type
L^{\prime},n^{\prime}

in
\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}(L^{\prime},n^{\prime})
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B^{k}_{D}(L,n)}\sum_{G\in\mathcal{B}^{k}_{L,n}}\int_{[0,1]^{E_{G}}}\frac{f\left(G,{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)}{|\Aut(G)|}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}\text{d}z_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{z}})^{D/2}}\,,

which proves the claim.
∎

Proof of Theorem
20
.

Follows immediately from Lemma
22

and Theorem
21
.
∎

Algorithms
18

and
19

only produce samples of pairs
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
with cubical edge coordinates
{\boldsymbol{z}}
. We can directly map these samples to samples that are distributed as the normalized tropical measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
as given in eq. (
22
):

Theorem 23
.

There is a canonical many-to-one map from
(0,1)^{E_{G}}

to
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}
by reinterpreting
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in(0,1)^{E_{G}}
as a set of homogeneous coordinates.
Sampling a pair
(G,{\boldsymbol{z}})
via Algorithm
18
and mapping the resulting element
{\boldsymbol{z}}
to
\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}
via this canonical map produces samples that are distributed as the normalized tropical measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
on the moduli space of
k
-regular graphs.

Proof.

Follows from Theorem
20
and the argument in Proposition
5
’s proof.
∎

The recursions in Algorithms
18
and
19
always reduce the total number of edges in the next lower rank of the recursion tree. The initial discrete random sampling step of Algorithm
19
takes constant time after a preprocessing step of runtime and memory requirements
\mathcal{O}(Ln)
using the alias method
[
40
, §3.4.1]
. The sampling step of the subset
S
in Algorithm
19
takes at most runtime
n_{\mathrm{max}}
, where
n_{\mathrm{max}}
is the maximal number of legs of a graph encountered in the course of the computation. This number is smaller than
2|E_{G}|+n
, where
|E_{G}|
and
n
are the number of edges and legs of the output graph. In total, we get that the number of computational steps grows at most as
|E_{G}|(2|E_{G}|+n)
. In our
k
-regular case,
|E_{G}|
is proportional to the number of loops of
G
. Hence, we can record the following on the runtime of
Algorithms
18

and
19
:

Theorem 24
.

The runtime of Algorithms
18

and
19

is at most of the order of
\mathcal{O}(L(L+n))
.

Running Algorithms
18
and
19
requires the computation of the normalization constants
Z_{D}^{k}(L,n)
and
B_{D}^{k}(L,n)
as described in Section
4.2
. As we explained there, these can be computed in runtime
\mathcal{O}(L^{2}(L+n)^{2})
and they require
\mathcal{O}(L(L+n))
memory. We thus have a polynomial-time and polynomial-memory algorithm for the sampling of the normalized tropical measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
on the moduli space of
k
-regular graphs.

5
Some illustrative applications

In this section, we will briefly discuss two concrete applications of the global sampling algorithm from the last section.
A proof-of-concept
C++
implementation of the algorithm is attached with the ancillary files to the
arXiv version of this article and available on GitHub.
2
2

2

https://github.com/michibo/amplitrop
All computations can be reproduced using this
implementation.
We leave a full, in-depth interpretation of the resulting data to future work.

5.1
Application to massive
\phi^{3}
theory in
D=3

The algorithm introduced in the last section samples graphs proportional to their contribution to the tropicalized perturbative expansion.
For that to be immediately applicable without further modifications, we need
all contributing (tropicalized) Feynman integrals to be finite. For scalar massive quantum field theories, this only holds in low dimensions
D
. For instance, we can use the algorithm to compute the
n
-point correlation functions in
\phi^{3}
theory in
D=3
for
n\geq 2
, as these
n
-point functions are finite and have no subdivergences:
the conditions for Proposition
15
are fulfilled with
k=3
and
D=3
.
Such computations of finite quantities in low-dimensional super-renormalizable quantum field theories have interesting phenomenological applications.
See, for instance,
[
51
]
for a recent exposition.

As an illustration of the algorithm’s capabilities, we evaluate the 1PI
3
-point correlation function with zero momentum transfer and with the mass parameter
m^{2}=1
. That means, we compute the coefficients

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,3}^{1}(0,0,0)

from (
21
)
in the special case
k=3
,
n=3
,
p_{1}=p_{2}=p_{3}=0
,
and
m^{2}/\mu^{2}=1
in (
13
),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}_{3}^{1}(0,0,0)=(4\pi)^{\frac{3}{4}}\sum_{L\geq 1}\left(\frac{\lambda_{3}}{(4\pi)^{\frac{3}{4}}}\right)^{2(L-1)+3}\widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,3}^{1}(0,0,0),

which are explicitly given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \widetilde{\Gamma}_{L,3}^{1}(0,0,0)&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}_{L,3}^{3}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Gamma(\omega_{3}(G))}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{3/2}\cdot\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{\omega_{3}(G)}}\Omega\,\\
&=Z^{3}_{3}(L,3)\cdot\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{3}_{L,3}}\Gamma(\omega_{3}(G)+1)\left(\frac{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{3/2}\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{\omega_{3}(G)}\mu^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,3}\,,\end{aligned}

(30)

where, as we are in the zero momentum case,

\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})=m^{2}/\mu^{2}\sum_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}=\sum_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}
.

Table 1
:
3
-point function computation in massive
\phi^{3}

.

Up to loop order 20, Table
1
lists the number of samples taken, the estimate for the coefficient, and the computing time in hours on all cores of a computer. The computation ran on various nodes of Perimeter Institute’s computing cluster. All these nodes were equipped with an
AMD EPYC 7532 32-Core Processor
.
At 20 loops, the memory requirement of the algorithm is of the order of a couple of kilobytes, which is negligible by 2025 standards. So, we did not record the memory usage.

Table
1
illustrates the favourable runtime scaling of the algorithm with increasing loop order. In the regime up to
L=20
, the algorithm’s runtime appears to scale as
\mathcal{O}(L^{\frac{3}{2}})
, faster than the estimates obtained for Theorem
24
.

The implementation uses the Eigen
[
36
]
linear algebra library to compute the Cholesky decomposition of the graph Laplacian whose determinant equals
\mathcal{U}_{G}
(see, e.g.,
[
19
, §4.2]
for an exposition of fast Feynman integrand evaluation methods). For larger values of
L
, the computation of this decomposition at each sampled point will become significant. It scales as
\mathcal{O}(L^{3})
.

We verified the computation for the first couple of loop orders using
feyntrop

[
19
]
.
However, we have to give a cautionary warning: as we will discuss in the next section, the reader should be careful while interpreting the data in Table
1
at larger loop orders
L
. Especially, the error intervals might be overly optimistic, as these are also estimated via a Monte Carlo process.

We will leave the interpretation and explicit application of such computations to future work.

5.2
Bounds on the estimates’ relative error

In Table
1
, we observe that the error bands for the obtained estimates grow significantly with the loop order. So, even though the global sampling algorithm and all preprocessing steps run in polynomial time, we need to sample a rapidly growing number of points with the
loop order to get a fixed relative accuracy for our
n
-point function estimate. In fact, the number of points we need to sample might grow exponentially or more. So, by obtaining a polynomial-time algorithm for the sampling of points over the moduli space with a bounded fluctuation term, we have not necessarily achieved a polynomial-time algorithm to evaluate the original perturbative coefficient to fixed relative accuracy.

This section briefly explains how to study this error term and the relative accuracy qualitatively by providing hard bounds on it. Suppose we want to estimate the expectation value of a random variable (i.e. a function)
f
on the moduli space
\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
via sampling from the tropical probability measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
. That means, we aim to estimate the integral

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]=\int_{\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}}f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\,\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}\,.

Provided that the variance,
\mathrm{Var}[f]=\mathbb{E}[f^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}[f])^{2}
is finite, we can estimate
\mathbb{E}[f]
by averaging over many evaluations of
f
on samples
(G_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,(G_{N},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{N})
, which are drawn independently using the measure
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,n}
. This is the typical Monte Carlo approach:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f]\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(G_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})\,.

The statistical error of this estimate follows a Gaussian distribution with mean
0
and variance
\mathrm{Var}[f]/N
by the central
limit theorem.
In our setup, we will only consider functions
f
that are bounded due to applications of Lemma
1
. So, the variance is always finite, the central limit theorem holds, and the overall Monte Carlo process is guaranteed to work.

However, to get concrete bounds on the
relative
error of the estimate, we also need a well-behaved lower bound for
f
. Suppose that we find (e.g., by using an argument similar to the one for Lemma
1
) that
f
is bounded by constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that
C_{1}\leq f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\leq C_{2}
for all
representatives of metric graphs
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})\in\mathcal{MG}^{k}_{L,n}
. Then the expected relative error is

\displaystyle\delta[f]=\frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[f]/N}}{\mathbb{E}[f]}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sqrt{\frac{\mathbb{E}[f^{2}]}{\mathbb{E}[f]^{2}}-1}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[f^{2}]}}{\mathbb{E}[f]}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\frac{C_{2}}{C_{1}}\,.

(31)

Notice that by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,
\mathbb{E}[f^{2}]\geq\mathbb{E}[f]^{2}
. The
obtained bound on the relative error in the last step is governed by the ratio of the lower and upper bounds on
f
.

As a concrete example, we will discuss the relative error for the estimates of our
\phi^{3}
theory
3
-point function in
D=3
above.
To get concrete error bounds, we need to analyze the integrand over the moduli space
\mathcal{MG}^{3}_{L,3}
in eq. (
30
).
From the argument for Lemma
1
, we get the bounds,

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{T}_{G}|}\leq\frac{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\leq 1\,,

where
|\mathcal{T}_{G}|
is the number of spanning trees of
G
, and

\frac{1}{|E_{G}|}\leq\frac{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}=\frac{\max_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}{\sum_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}\leq 1\,.

In both cases, the upper bounds look promising. Unfortunately, the lower bounds can get tiny.

Recall (e.g. from Lemma
14
) that for a
L
loop 3-legged 3-regular graph
G
, we have
|E_{G}|=3L
and
\omega_{3}(G)=\frac{3}{2}L
.
Moreover, a graph has at most as many spanning trees
as it has edge-subgraphs. So,
|\mathcal{T}_{G}|\leq 2^{|E_{G}|}
.
For the integration kernel

f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\Gamma(\omega_{3}(G)+1)\left(\frac{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{3/2}\left(\frac{\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{\omega_{3}(G)}\,,

we find, hence, that in total

\delta[f]\leq(24L)^{\frac{3}{2}L}
by eq. (
31
).

Unfortunately, this bound grows more than factorially with
L
. The source of this huge growth rate is the term

\left({\mathcal{V}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}/{\mathcal{V}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{\omega_{3}(G)}

in the moduli space integrand (
30
). In particular, the exponent
\omega_{3}(G)
that grows with the loop order leads to a super-exponentially growing bound.

In the practical computation listed in Table
1
, the error intervals do not grow as much as this theoretical bound on
\delta[f]
indicates. In fact, the error only seems to grow exponentially with the loop order. Still, the growth of the variance obstructs the study of loop orders in massive scalar
\phi^{3}
theory in
D=3
beyond
\approx 20
using the present approach without any modifications.

Salvatori discusses variance reduction techniques in the context of sampling of surface integrals
[
50
, §5]
. Plausibly, importing those ideas to the present context can lead to improvements of our estimates and our theoretical bounds in their errors.

Further, the exponent
\omega_{3}(G)
only grows because we chose to study a super-renormalizable quantum field theory. In a renormalizable quantum field theory, this exponent would remain constant, and we can expect a much better theoretical bound on the relative error.

In the next section, we will therefore sketch a way to go forward and also perform computations in quantum field theories in the critical dimension using a slight modification of the algorithm from Section
4
.

5.3
Renormalization and the positive Hepp bound

In this section, we introduce an extension of the algorithm from Section
4
that allows us to evaluate certain perturbative objects in higher-dimensional quantum field theories where divergences appear
and the conditions for Proposition
15
are not fulfilled anymore. The standard tool to manage divergences in quantum field theory is renormalization.
Because we only consider a massive scalar field here, all relevant divergences are UV divergences.

Definition 25
.

The positive Hepp bound
\mathcal{H}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G)
is defined for
each graph
G
by setting

\mathcal{H}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G)=1
if
G
is a graph without edges,
by

\mathcal{H}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(H)=\prod_{k}\mathcal{H}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G_{k})

for a non-1PI graph
H
with 1PI components
G_{1},\ldots,G_{n}
where
n\geq 2

and otherwise if
G
is a 1PI graph,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\omega_{D}(G)}\sum_{e\in E_{G}}{\mathcal{H}}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G\setminus e)&\text{ if }\omega_{D}(G)>0\\
0&\text{ else}\,,\end{cases}

which recursively defines the positive Hepp bound for all graphs.

The positive Hepp bound is not a rational function in
D
, and it can have quite complicated singularities in
D
.
We remark that the positive Hepp bound is a renormalized version of the original Hepp bound from Definition
3
. This can be proven using the Connes-Kreimer interpretation of renormalization
[
29
,
30
]
(see also
[
46
]
).

For fixed
D
, we define the generating function of the positive Hepp bound as a renormalized version of the tropical effective action (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathrm{pos}}=\sum_{G\in\mathcal{G}}\frac{\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}}{|\Aut^{\prime}(G)|}\,{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{pos}}_{D}(G)\,.\end{gathered}

The argument in Section
3
can be applied transparently to give a fixed-point equation for the positive Hepp bound. The result is

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathrm{pos}}=\sum_{k\geq 3}\varphi^{k}\frac{\lambda_{k}}{k!}+\mathcal{P}_{D}^{-1}\circ(\id-K_{D})\circ\left(\left(1-\frac{\partial^{2}\Gamma^{\mathrm{tr}}_{\mathrm{pos}}}{\partial\varphi^{2}}\right)^{-1}-1\right),

where
\id
is the identity operator and

K_{D}
is the operator that acts on basis monomials that correspond to 1PI graphs by mapping

K_{D}(\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|})=\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|}
if
\omega(G)<0
and
K_{D}(\varphi^{n(G)}\prod_{v\in V_{G}}\lambda_{|v|})=0
if
\omega(G)\geq 0
. In our setup,
K_{D}
takes the role of the operator isolating the divergent contribution that is typically used in the BPHZ formalism. In fact, it is possible
(but very inefficient) to compute
{\mathcal{H}}_{D}^{\mathrm{pos}}(G)
from
{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)
using the BPHZ formalism.

Replacing
{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)
with
{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{pos}}_{D}(G)
in Section
4
yields an algorithm that samples pairs
(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})
of metric graphs weighted by the positive Hepp bound
{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{pos}}_{D}(G)
. By Definition
25
,
it is obvious that
{\mathcal{H}}_{D}(G)={\mathcal{H}}^{\mathrm{pos}}_{D}(G)
if
\omega_{D}(\gamma)>0
for all subgraphs
\gamma\subset G
. That means, for graphs without subdivergences,
the positive Hepp bound and the original Hepp bound are equal.
By definition, the positive Hepp bound is
always non-negative and finite. So, this modified sampling algorithm is not obstructed by issues of potential divergences.

5.4
Application to the primitive
\phi^{4}
theory
\beta
function

We will apply this modified algorithm based on the positive Hepp bound to compute a particularly interesting quantity in scalar quantum field theory: the primitive contribution to the scalar
\phi^{4}
theory
\beta
function
[
21
,
22
,
52
]
. This quantity has long served as a benchmark for new computational techniques. While it is known analytically up to 7 loops, at loop order 8, new number-theoretic phenomena emerge, which pose obstructions for an evaluation using existing analytic methods
[
26
]
.
With Hepp-bound-based methods Kompaniets and Panzer
estimated this primitive contribution up to 11 loops
[
41
]
.
Recently, Balduf used tropical sampling to study the primitive contribution to the
\phi^{4}
theory
\beta
function up to loop order 18
[
8
]
(see also
[
10
,
11
]
). Up to 17 loops, these computations were later confirmed by Favorito and the author
[
17
]
.
A folklore conjecture states that the primitive contribution to the minimally subtracted
\phi^{4}
beta function becomes dominant at large loop order
[
43
]
(see also
[
33
]
for an illuminating discussion).

A
4
-point graph in
\phi^{4}
theory,
G\in\mathcal{G}_{L,4}^{4}
, is
primitive if it is logarithmically divergent for
D=4
(meaning that
\omega_{4}(G)=0
) and free of subdivergences (meaning that
\omega_{4}(\gamma)>0
for all subgraphs
\gamma\subset G
).
The primitive contribution to the
\beta
function in
\phi^{4}
theory is (see
[
41
, Eq. (B1)]
),

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}G\in\mathcal{G}_{L,4}^{4}\\
\end{subarray}}\frac{\Theta(G\text{ is primitive})}{|\Aut(G)|}\int_{\mathbb{P}_{>0}^{E_{G}}}\frac{\prod_{e\in E_{G}}x_{e}}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})^{2}}\,\Omega\,,

where
\Theta(G\text{ is primitive})
is
1
if
G
is primitive and
0
otherwise.
We can use the positive versions of the Algorithms
18
and
19
to
estimate
\beta^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}
by writing it in the form

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}=2Z^{4,\mathrm{pos}}_{4}(L,4)\int_{\mathcal{M}_{L,4}^{4}}\Theta(G\text{ is primitive})\,\left(\frac{\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{2}\,\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr},\mathrm{pos}}_{L,4}\,,

where
Z^{4,\mathrm{pos}}_{4}(L,4)
and
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr},\mathrm{pos}}_{L,4}
are the positive versions
of the normalization factor and measure

Z^{4}_{4}(L,4)
and
\widetilde{\mu}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{L,4}

from Sections
4.1
and
4.2
.

Table 2
:
Primitive
\beta
function computation in
\phi^{4}
theory.

The positive modifications of Algorithms
18
and
19
are implemented in the
C++
code that is attached to the paper. In fact, the positive version of the algorithm is equivalent to the original one up to a check of positivity of
\omega^{k}_{D}(L,n)
in the recursion step (
28
). The results of a single pilot-study computation on the Perimeter Institute’s computing cluster are listed in Table
2
, which contains the number of samples, the number of samples where
G
was primitive
N_{\mathrm{prim}}
,
\beta^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}
, the runtime of the computation in hours and the value of

\beta H^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}
, which is the Hepp-version of the primitive
\beta
function in
\phi^{4}
theory:

\displaystyle\beta H^{\mathrm{prim}}_{L+1}=2Z^{4,\mathrm{pos}}_{4}(L,4)\int_{\mathcal{M}_{L,4}^{4}}\Theta(G\text{ is primitive})\,\mu^{\mathrm{tr},\mathrm{pos}}_{L,4}\,.

The main bottleneck of the computation is to check if a graph is primitive or not. This check can be performed in polynomial time by enumerating all
4
-edge cuts of the graph and checking if the respective cut splits the graph into two non-trivial components. However, the number of potential cuts grows as
|E_{G}|^{4}
; faster than the runtime of any other component of the computation.

The timings in Table
2
are not fully representative, as two different types of computers, one with an
AMD EPYC 7532 32-Core Processor
and one with a
Intel Xeon Gold 6148 CPU
were used for the computation.
Again, we did not record the memory usage, as the core memory requirements of the algorithm would still be on the order of 200 kilobytes at loop order 100.
The overall memory consumption is therefore dominated by operating system overhead.

The integrand
f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})=\left({\mathcal{U}_{G}^{\mathrm{tr}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}/{\mathcal{U}_{G}({\boldsymbol{x}})}\right)^{2}
is much better-behaved than the integrand in the super-renormalizable case discussed above in Section
5.1
. As expected, the errors grow more slowly with the loop order than before in our
\phi^{3}
theory example (see Table
2
). We obtained estimates with a reasonable statistical error bound up to loop order 50. At loop orders 18 and below, the numbers confirm and refine results from
[
8
,
10
,
17
]
. We emphasize that the new computations presented here were carried out in a fraction of the time required for the more basic, uniform graph sampling approach of these papers.

However, the statistical errors still seem to grow exponentially with the loop order. This growth can be explained as follows. The integrand,
f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})
, is always smaller than
1
, and it can become arbitrarily small at large enough loop order. The crude lower bound,
{2^{-2|E_{G}|}}\leq f(G,{\boldsymbol{x}})
obtained by estimating the number of spanning trees with the total number of subgraphs, can only be improved to a better, but still exponentially
decreasing, bound (see
[
44
]
). Hence,
the discussion in Section
5.2
shows that we end up with an exponentially growing bound on the relative accuracy of our estimate
\delta[f]\leq 2^{4L}
, where we used that a
\phi^{4}
theory
4
-point graph with
L
loops has
2L
edges.
We therefore proved that we have an algorithm
with which we can compute the primitive
\phi^{4}
theory
\beta
function at
L
loops up to
\delta
relative accuracy in runtime
\mathcal{O}(L^{4}\delta^{-CL})
, where
C
is a constant
>0
. So, requiring fixed relative accuracy, still results in an exponential time algorithm in
L
.
As before, this bound and estimate are overly pessimistic, but in line with the empirical observation that the errors grow exponentially. We hope that systematic improvements of the global sampling algorithm will lead to a polynomial-time algorithm for the full estimation of the
L
-loop perturbative coefficient at fixed relative accuracy.

6
Perspectives & generalizations

In this section, we summarize several perspectives and possible generalizations that emerged from the open directions highlighted throughout the article.

•

Except for the brief excursions in Sections
3.4
and
5.3
, this paper focused exclusively on
bare tropicalized quantum field theory
. An exciting direction for future work is to investigate the renormalization group properties of tropicalized quantum field theory itself (see
[
9
]
).

Furthermore, it would be extremely valuable to incorporate renormalization directly into the sampling algorithm described in Section
4
, to compute renormalized
n
-point correlation functions and other quantities in situ. Given the numerical nature of the method, divergences would need to be addressed locally at the integrand level, so that only finite quantities remain to be integrated. Recent progress in local subtraction schemes (see
[
25
,
38
,
49
]
) should be particularly helpful while designing a fully renormalized algorithm.

•

There are many ways to generalize the underlying quantum field theory beyond massive scalar fields. Natural first steps include massless scalar quantum field theories and theories with multiple scalar fields. Further interesting directions would be the tropicalization of quantum field theories with tensor structures, and eventually the tropicalization of gauge theories and theories with fermions.

•

For applications of the global tropical sampling algorithm from Section
4
to collider physics, it would be interesting to combine the algorithm from the present paper with that of
[
18
]
to directly sample pairs of a graph and the associated loop momenta along its edges. Such a combination could also prove useful for the evaluation of phase-space integrals and simulating particle scattering processes.

•

Conceptually, the sampling method over the moduli space of graphs could provide a way to study the number-theoretic properties of Feynman integrals (see
[
23
]
) quantitatively. Interesting questions include, for example, what proportion of the contributing integrals can be expressed in terms of generalized hyperlogarithms, and which transcendental weights dominate the contributions.

•

Furthermore, it is plausible that the algorithm could be significantly improved using systematic variance-reduction techniques. In particular, it would be interesting to achieve a rigorous reduction of the variance such that the Monte Carlo application of the algorithm maintains polynomial complexity in the loop order, multiplicity, and target accuracy.
